B. 6

D. 9

请将答案放在 \boxed{} 中。
Response
**逐步推理：**
1. **数据排序**：求中位数前，必须先将样本数据按从小到大的顺序排列。原数据为 `6, 8, 4, 5, 12`，排序后为：`4, 5, 6, 8, 12`。
2. **确定位置**：该样本共有 5 个数据，为奇数。根据中位数的定义，当数据个数为奇数时，中位数是排序后正中间的那个数，其位置为第 $\frac{5+1}{2}=3$ 位。
3. **取值**：查看排序后列表的第 3 个数，为 `6`。

因此，该样本数据的中位数为 6，对应选项 B。

\boxed{B}

Prompt
请解答以下数学题（单选题）。逐步推理，将最终答案放在 \boxed{} 中。

2. 已知平面向量\vec{a}，\vec{b}不共线，且2\vec{a} + y\vec{b} = x\vec{a} - 3\vec{b}，则（ ）
A. x=2, y=-3
B. x=-2, y=3
C. x=2, y=3
D. x=-2, y=-3

请将答案放在 \boxed{} 中。
Response
**逐步推理：**

1. **理解题设条件**：已知平面向量 $\vec{a}$ 与 $\vec{b}$ **不共线**。根据平面向量基本定理，平面内任意两个不共线的向量都可以作为该平面的一组基底。这意味着平面内的任一向量用这组基底线性表示时，表示形式是**唯一**的。

2. **列出等式并整理**：题目给出向量等式：
   $$2\vec{a} + y\vec{b} = x\vec{a} - 3\vec{b}$$

3. **利用基底表示的唯一性**：由于 $\vec{a}$ 和 $\vec{b}$ 不共线（线性无关），等式两边对应基向量的系数必须分别相等。
   - 比较 $\vec{a}$ 的系数：左边为 $2$，右边为 $x$，故 $x = 2$。
   - 比较 $\vec{b}$ 的系数：左边为 $y$，右边为 $-3$，故 $y = -3$。

4. **得出结论**：解得 $x=2$，$y=-3$。对照选项，与选项 A 完全一致。

\boxed{A}
Reasoning
Here's a thinking process:

1.  **Analyze User Input:**
   - **Question Type:** Multiple choice question (单选题)
   - **Given:** Two planar vectors $\vec{a}$ and $\vec{b}$ are not collinear (不共线).
   - **Equation:** $2\vec{a} + y\vec{b} = x\vec{a} - 3\vec{b}$
   - **Options:**
     A. $x=2, y=-3$
     B. $x=-2, y=3$
     C. $x=2, y=3$
     D. $x=-2, y=-3$
   - **Task:** Solve for $x$ and $y$, and put the final answer in `\boxed{}`.

2.  **Identify Key Mathematical Concepts:**
   - Vector equality: Two vectors are equal if and only if their corresponding components are equal. In terms of a basis, if $\vec{a}$ and $\vec{b}$ are linearly independent (不共线 means non-collinear, which in 2D implies they form a basis), then any vector can be uniquely expressed as a linear combination of $\vec{a}$ and $\vec{b}$.
   - Therefore, if $c_1 \vec{a} + c_2 \vec{b} = d_1 \vec{a} + d_2 \vec{b}$ and $\vec{a}, \vec{b}$ are linearly independent, then $c_1 = d_1$ and $c_2 = d_2$.

3.  **Apply Concepts to the Problem:**
   - Given: $2\vec{a} + y\vec{b} = x\vec{a} - 3\vec{b}$
   - Rearrange/Compare coefficients of $\vec{a}$ and $\vec{b}$:
     - Coefficient of $\vec{a}$: $2 = x$
     - Coefficient of $\vec{b}$: $y = -3$
   - So, $x = 2$, $y = -3$.

4.  **Match with Options:**
   - A. $x=2, y=-3$ -> Matches.
   - B. $x=-2, y=3$
   - C. $x=2, y=3$
   - D. $x=-2, y=-3$
   - Correct option is A.

5.  **Formulate Step-by-Step Reasoning (for output):**
   - State the given condition: $\vec{a}$ and $\vec{b}$ are non-collinear planar vectors, which means they are linearly independent and can serve as a basis for the plane.
   - Use the property of basis vectors: If $m_1\vec{a} + n_1\vec{b} = m_2\vec{a} + n_2\vec{b}$, then $m_1 = m_2$ and $n_1 = n_2$.
   - Apply to the equation: $2\vec{a} + y\vec{b} = x\vec{a} - 3\vec{b}$.
   - Compare coefficients:
     - For $\vec{a}$: $2 = x \Rightarrow x = 2$.
     - For $\vec{b}$: $y = -3 \Rightarrow y = -3$.
   - Conclude: $x=2, y=-3$, which corresponds to option A.
   - Format the final answer as requested.

6.  **Self-Correction/Verification:**
   - Check logic: Non-collinear vectors in a plane are linearly independent. The representation of any vector in terms of a basis is unique. So equating coefficients is valid.
   - Check calculation: $2=x$, $y=-3$. Correct.
   - Check options: A matches.
   - Format: Step-by-step reasoning followed by `\boxed{A}`.
   - All good.

gaokao_000_002 ✓ C C 2783 59.5 46.8 http://192.168.0.41:9292
Prompt
请解答以下数学题（单选题）。逐步推理，将最终答案放在 \boxed{} 中。

3. 已知集合A={sin\frac{7\pi}{6}, cos\frac{5\pi}{3}, tan\frac{5\pi}{4}}, B={-\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}, -\frac{1}{2}, 1}，则A \cap B = （ ）
A. {-\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}, -\frac{1}{2}}
B. {-\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}, 1}
C. {-\frac{1}{2}, 1}
D. {-\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}, -\frac{1}{2}, 1}

请将答案放在 \boxed{} 中。
Response
**逐步推理：**

1. **计算集合 $A$ 中的元素值：**
   - $\sin\frac{7\pi}{6} = \sin(\pi + \frac{\pi}{6}) = -\sin\frac{\pi}{6} = -\frac{1}{2}$
   - $\cos\frac{5\pi}{3} = \cos(2\pi - \frac{\pi}{3}) = \cos\frac{\pi}{3} = \frac{1}{2}$
   - $\tan\frac{5\pi}{4} = \tan(\pi + \frac{\pi}{4}) = \tan\frac{\pi}{4} = 1$
   因此，集合 $A = \left\{-\frac{1}{2}, \frac{1}{2}, 1\right\}$。

2. **对比集合 $B$：**
   已知集合 $B = \left\{-\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}, -\frac{1}{2}, 1\right\}$。

3. **求交集 $A \cap B$：**
   交集由同时属于 $A$ 和 $B$ 的元素组成。逐一比对：
   - $-\frac{1}{2}$ 在 $A$ 和 $B$ 中均有；
   - $1$ 在 $A$ 和 $B$ 中均有；
   - $\frac{1}{2}$ 仅在 $A$ 中；
   - $-\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}$ 仅在 $B$ 中。
   故 $A \cap B = \left\{-\frac{1}{2}, 1\right\}$。

4. **对照选项：**
   计算结果与选项 C 完全一致。

\boxed{C}
Reasoning
Here's a thinking process:

1.  **Analyze User Input:**
   - **Question Type:** Multiple choice question (单选题)
   - **Problem:** Given sets $A = \{\sin\frac{7\pi}{6}, \cos\frac{5\pi}{3}, \tan\frac{5\pi}{4}\}$ and $B = \{-\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}, -\frac{1}{2}, 1\}$, find $A \cap B$.
   - **Options:**
     A. $\{-\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}, -\frac{1}{2}\}$
     B. $\{-\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}, 1\}$
     C. $\{-\frac{1}{2}, 1\}$
     D. $\{-\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}, -\frac{1}{2}, 1\}$
   - **Requirement:** Step-by-step reasoning, final answer in `\boxed{}`.

2.  **Evaluate Elements of Set A:**
   - Need to calculate each trigonometric value:
     - $\sin\frac{7\pi}{6}$: $\frac{7\pi}{6}$ is in the 3rd quadrant. Reference angle is $\frac{\pi}{6}$. Sine is negative in Q3. So $\sin\frac{7\pi}{6} = -\sin\frac{\pi}{6} = -\frac{1}{2}$.
     - $\cos\frac{5\pi}{3}$: $\frac{5\pi}{3}$ is in the 4th quadrant. Reference angle is $\frac{\pi}{3}$. Cosine is positive in Q4. So $\cos\frac{5\pi}{3} = \cos\frac{\pi}{3} = \frac{1}{2}$. Wait, let me double check. $\cos(300^\circ) = \cos(60^\circ) = 1/2$. Yes.
     - $\tan\frac{5\pi}{4}$: $\frac{5\pi}{4}$ is in the 3rd quadrant. Reference angle is $\frac{\pi}{4}$. Tangent is positive in Q3. So $\tan\frac{5\pi}{4} = \tan\frac{\pi}{4} = 1$.
   - So, $A = \{-\frac{1}{2}, \frac{1}{2}, 1\}$.

3.  **Compare with Set B:**
   - $B = \{-\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}, -\frac{1}{2}, 1\}$.
   - Find intersection $A \cap B$: elements common to both sets.
   - Elements in A: $-\frac{1}{2}, \frac{1}{2}, 1$.
   - Elements in B: $-\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}, -\frac{1}{2}, 1$.
   - Common elements: $-\frac{1}{2}$ and $1$.
   - So, $A \cap B = \{-\frac{1}{2}, 1\}$.

4.  **Match with Options:**
   - A. $\{-\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}, -\frac{1}{2}\}$
   - B. $\{-\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}, 1\}$
   - C. $\{-\frac{1}{2}, 1\}$
   - D. $\{-\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}, -\frac{1}{2}, 1\}$
   - The calculated intersection matches Option C.

5.  **Double Check Calculations:**
   - $\sin(7\pi/6) = \sin(210^\circ) = -1/2$. Correct.
   - $\cos(5\pi/3) = \cos(300^\circ) = 1/2$. Correct.
   - $\tan(5\pi/4) = \tan(225^\circ) = 1$. Correct.
   - $A = \{-1/2, 1/2, 1\}$.
   - $B = \{-\sqrt{3}/2, -1/2, 1\}$.

gaokao_000_003 ✓ D D 2308 56.8 40.7 http://192.168.0.41:9292
Prompt
请解答以下数学题（单选题）。逐步推理，将最终答案放在 \boxed{} 中。

4. 曲线y=5x+8lnx在点(1,5)处的切线方程为（ ）
A. y=3x+2
B. y=5x
C. y=8x-3
D. y=13x-8

请将答案放在 \boxed{} 中。
Response
要解决这个问题，我们按照求曲线切线方程的标准步骤逐步推理：

**步骤 1：求函数的导数**
曲线方程为 $y = 5x + 8\ln x$。
根据求导法则，对 $x$ 求导得：
$$y' = (5x)' + (8\ln x)' = 5 + \frac{8}{x}$$

**步骤 2：求切点处的切线斜率**
已知切点为 $(1, 5)$，将横坐标 $x = 1$ 代入导数表达式，计算该点处的切线斜率 $k$：
$$k = y'\big|_{x=1} = 5 + \frac{8}{1} = 13$$

**步骤 3：利用点斜式写出切线方程**
直线的点斜式方程为 $y - y_0 = k(x - x_0)$。代入切点 $(1, 5)$ 和斜率 $k = 13$：
$$y - 5 = 13(x - 1)$$

**步骤 4：化简方程**
展开并整理为斜截式：
$$y - 5 = 13x - 13$$
$$y = 13x - 8$$

**步骤 5：对照选项**
化简后的切线方程 $y = 13x - 8$ 与选项 D 完全一致。

因此，正确答案为 D。

\boxed{D}

     - The slope of the tangent line, which is $f'(x_0)$.

Prompt

5. 已知抛物线C_1:y^2=2p_1x (p_1>0)和C_2:x^2=2p_2y (p_2>0)均经过点(4,8)，则C_1的焦点与C_2的焦点之间的距离为（ ）
A. 12
B. 4\sqrt{5}
C. 6
D. \frac{\sqrt{65}}{2}

请将答案放在 \boxed{} 中。
Response
**逐步推理过程：**

1. **确定抛物线焦点坐标公式**
   * 对于开口向右的抛物线 $C_1: y^2 = 2p_1x$ $(p_1>0)$，其焦点坐标为 $F_1\left(\frac{p_1}{2}, 0\right)$。
   * 对于开口向上的抛物线 $C_2: x^2 = 2p_2y$ $(p_2>0)$，其焦点坐标为 $F_2\left(0, \frac{p_2}{2}\right)$。

2. **利用已知点求参数 $p_1$ 和 $p_2$**
   * 将点 $(4,8)$ 代入 $C_1$ 的方程：
     $$8^2 = 2p_1 \times 4 \implies 64 = 8p_1 \implies p_1 = 8$$
     因此，$C_1$ 的焦点坐标为 $F_1\left(\frac{8}{2}, 0\right) = (4, 0)$。
   * 将点 $(4,8)$ 代入 $C_2$ 的方程：
     $$4^2 = 2p_2 \times 8 \implies 16 = 16p_2 \implies p_2 = 1$$
     因此，$C_2$ 的焦点坐标为 $F_2\left(0, \frac{1}{2}\right)$。

3. **计算两焦点之间的距离**
   根据平面直角坐标系中两点间距离公式：
   $$|F_1F_2| = \sqrt{(x_2 - x_1)^2 + (y_2 - y_1)^2} = \sqrt{(0 - 4)^2 + \left(\frac{1}{2} - 0\right)^2}$$
   $$= \sqrt{16 + \frac{1}{4}} = \sqrt{\frac{64}{4} + \frac{1}{4}} = \sqrt{\frac{65}{4}} = \frac{\sqrt{65}}{2}$$

4. **对照选项**
   计算结果 $\frac{\sqrt{65}}{2}$ 与选项 D 完全一致。

\boxed{D}

Prompt

6. 已知函数f(x)=\frac{x+2}{e^x+a}的最大值为1，则a = （ ）
A. \frac{1}{2}
B. 1
C. \frac{3}{2}
D. 2

请将答案放在 \boxed{} 中。
Response
**逐步推理过程：**

1. **求导分析单调性与极值点**
   函数为 $f(x)=\frac{x+2}{e^x+a}$。对 $f(x)$ 求导：
   $$f'(x) = \frac{1\cdot(e^x+a) - (x+2)\cdot e^x}{(e^x+a)^2} = \frac{a - (x+1)e^x}{(e^x+a)^2}$$
   令 $f'(x)=0$，得极值点满足的条件：
   $$(x+1)e^x = a \quad \cdots ①$$
   设方程①的唯一解为 $x_0$（由选项知 $a>0$，函数 $g(x)=(x+1)e^x$ 在 $x>-1$ 时单调递增，故解唯一）。分析导数符号可知，当 $x<x_0$ 时 $f'(x)>0$，当 $x>x_0$ 时 $f'(x)<0$，因此 $x_0$ 为函数 $f(x)$ 的极大值点，也是全局最大值点。

2. **利用最大值为1列方程**
   由题意知最大值为 1，即 $f(x_0)=1$：
   $$\frac{x_0+2}{e^{x_0}+a} = 1 \implies x_0+2 = e^{x_0} + a$$
   整理得：
   $$a = x_0 + 2 - e^{x_0} \quad \cdots ②$$

3. **联立方程求解**
   将②代入①中：
   $$(x_0+1)e^{x_0} = x_0 + 2 - e^{x_0}$$
   移项合并同类项：
   $$(x_0+1)e^{x_0} + e^{x_0} = x_0 + 2$$
   $$(x_0+2)e^{x_0} = x_0 + 2$$
   若 $x_0+2=0$，则 $x_0=-2$，代入①得 $a=(-1)e^{-2}<0$，与选项矛盾，故 $x_0+2 \neq 0$。两边同除以 $x_0+2$ 得：
   $$e^{x_0} = 1 \implies x_0 = 0$$
   将 $x_0=0$ 代入①求 $a$：
   $$a = (0+1)e^0 = 1$$

4. **验证**
   当 $a=1$ 时，$f(x)=\frac{x+2}{e^x+1}$。
   $f(0)=\frac{2}{1+1}=1$，且由前述导数分析知 $x=0$ 为唯一极大值点，结合 $\lim_{x\to-\infty}f(x)=-\infty$ 与 $\lim_{x\to+\infty}f(x)=0$，可知全局最大值确为 1，符合题意。

综上所述，$a=1$，对应选项 B。

\boxed{B}
Reasoning
Here's a thinking process:

1.  **Analyze the User Input:**
   - **Problem:** Given function $f(x) = \frac{x+2}{e^x + a}$ has a maximum value of 1. Find $a$.
   - **Options:** A. $1/2$, B. $1$, C. $3/2$, D. $2$
   - **Task:** Solve step-by-step, put the final answer in `\boxed{}`.

2.  **Understand the Function & Condition:**
   - $f(x) = \frac{x+2}{e^x + a}$
   - Maximum value is 1. This means $\max_{x \in \mathbb{R}} f(x) = 1$.
   - We need to find the parameter $a$ that satisfies this condition.
   - Note: The domain is likely all real numbers, assuming $e^x + a \neq 0$. Since $e^x > 0$, if $a > 0$, the denominator is always positive. If $a \le 0$, there might be a vertical asymptote. Given the options are all positive, $a > 0$ is safe. So domain is $\mathbb{R}$.

3.  **Find the Maximum Value Condition:**
   - To find the maximum, take the derivative $f'(x)$ and set it to 0.
   - $f(x) = \frac{u}{v}$ where $u = x+2$, $v = e^x + a$.
   - $f'(x) = \frac{u'v - uv'}{v^2} = \frac{1 \cdot (e^x + a) - (x+2) \cdot e^x}{(e^x + a)^2}$
   - Simplify numerator: $e^x + a - (x+2)e^x = e^x(1 - x - 2) + a = e^x(-x - 1) + a = a - (x+1)e^x$.
   - Set $f'(x) = 0 \Rightarrow a - (x+1)e^x = 0 \Rightarrow (x+1)e^x = a$.
   - Let $g(x) = (x+1)e^x$. We need to solve $g(x) = a$ for critical points.
   - Analyze $g(x)$: $g'(x) = e^x + (x+1)e^x = (x+2)e^x$.
   - $g'(x) = 0 \Rightarrow x = -2$.
   - For $x < -2$, $g'(x) < 0$ (decreasing).
   - For $x > -2$, $g'(x) > 0$ (increasing).
   - Minimum of $g(x)$ is at $x = -2$: $g(-2) = (-2+1)e^{-2} = -e^{-2} < 0$.
   - As $x \to -\infty$, $g(x) \to 0$ (from negative side? Let's check: $(x+1)e^x \to 0^-$). Actually, $\lim_{x\to-\infty} (x+1)e^x = 0$.
   - As $x \to +\infty$, $g(x) \to +\infty$.
   - Since $a > 0$ (from options), the equation $(x+1)e^x = a$ has exactly one real solution, say $x_0$, and since $a>0$, $x_0 > -1$ (because $g(x) < 0$ for $x < -1$, $g(-1)=0$, $g(x)>0$ for $x>-1$).
   - At $x_0$, $f'(x)$ changes from positive to negative? Let's check sign of $f'(x)$: numerator is $a - g(x)$. Since $g(x)$ is increasing for $x > -2$, and $x_0 > -1 > -2$, $g(x)$ is increasing at $x_0$. So for $x < x_0$, $g(x) < a \Rightarrow f'(x) > 0$. For $x > x_0$, $g(x) > a \Rightarrow f'(x) < 0$. Thus, $x_0$ is a global maximum point.
   - So, $f(x_0) = 1$.
   - We have two conditions at $x_0$:
     1. $(x_0+1)e^{x_0} = a$
     2. $f(x_0) = \frac{x_0+2}{e^{x_0} + a} = 1$

4.  **Solve the System of Equations:**
   - From (2): $x_0 + 2 = e^{x_0} + a \Rightarrow a = x_0 + 2 - e^{x_0}$.
   - Substitute $a$ into (1): $(x_0+1)e^{x_0} = x_0 + 2 - e^{x_0}$.
   - Rearrange: $(x_0+1)e^{x_0} + e^{x_0} = x_0 + 2 \Rightarrow (x_0+2)e^{x_0} = x_0 + 2$.
   - Since $x_0 + 2 \neq 0$ (if $x_0 = -2$, $a = -e^{-2} < 0$, contradiction with $a>0$), we can divide by $x_0 + 2$:
     $e^{x_0} = 1 \Rightarrow x_0 = 0$.
   - Check if $x_0 = 0$ is valid: $x_0 > -1$, yes.
   - Now find $a$: From $a = x_0 + 2 - e^{x_0} = 0 + 2 - e^0 = 2 - 1 = 1$.
   - Alternatively, from $(x_0+1)e^{x_0} = a \Rightarrow (0+1)e^0 = 1 \Rightarrow a = 1$.
   - So $a = 1$.

5.  **Verify the Result:**
   - If $a = 1$, $f(x) = \frac{x+2}{e^x + 1}$.
   - Find max: $f'(x) = \frac{1 - (x+1)e^x}{(e^x+1)^2}$. Set to 0 $\Rightarrow (x+1)e^x = 1$.
   - Solution is $x=0$ since $(0+1)e^0 = 1$.
   - $f(0) = \frac{0+2}{e^0+1} = \frac{2}{2} = 1$.
   - Check second derivative or sign change: For $x<0$, say $x=-1$, numerator $1 - 0 = 1 > 0$. For $x>0$, say $x=1$, numerator $1 - 2e \approx 1 - 5.43 < 0$. So $x=0$ is a maximum.
   - Limit at $\pm \infty$: $\lim_{x\to-\infty} f(x) = \lim_{x\to-\infty} \frac{x+2}{1} = -\infty$. $\lim_{x\to+\infty} f(x) = 0$ (by L'Hopital or growth rates). So global max is indeed 1.
   - Matches option B.

6.  **Structure the Step-by-Step Reasoning:**
   - State the function and condition.

Prompt
请解答以下数学题（单选题）。逐步推理，将最终答案放在 \boxed{} 中。

7. 一百零八塔位于宁夏回族自治区青铜峡市，以其独特的建筑格局和深厚的历史文化闻名遐迩。该塔群共有108座塔，依山势自上而下排成12行，将第i行中塔的座数记为a_i(i=1,2,...,12)，其中a_1=1, a_2=a_3=3, a_4=a_5=5，且a_6, a_7, ..., a_{12}是一个首项为7，公差为2的等差数列。将a_1, a_2, ..., a_{12}分为6组，每组2个数，使得每组的2个数之和可构成一个项数为6且公差为d(d>0)的等差数列，则d = （ ）
A. 2
B. 4
C. 6
D. 8

请将答案放在 \boxed{} 中。
Response
**逐步推理过程：**

1. **列出数列 $\{a_i\}$ 的所有项并求总和**
   根据题意：
   - $a_1 = 1$
   - $a_2 = a_3 = 3$
   - $a_4 = a_5 = 5$
   - $a_6, a_7, \dots, a_{12}$ 是首项为 $7$、公差为 $2$ 的等差数列，共 $7$ 项，依次为：$7, 9, 11, 13, 15, 17, 19$。
   
   将这 $12$ 个数放在一起得到集合：$\{1, 3, 3, 5, 5, 7, 9, 11, 13, 15, 17, 19\}$。
   它们的总和为：$1 + 3\times2 + 5\times2 + (7+9+11+13+15+17+19) = 1 + 6 + 10 + 91 = 108$（与题目“一百零八塔”的总数吻合）。

2. **建立等差数列和的方程**
   将这 $12$ 个数分为 $6$ 组，每组 $2$ 个数。设每组的和依次为 $S_1, S_2, \dots, S_6$。
   由题意，这 $6$ 个和构成公差为 $d\ (d>0)$ 的等差数列。
   根据等差数列求和公式，这 $6$ 个和的总和为：
   $$6S_1 + \frac{6\times5}{2}d = 6S_1 + 15d$$
   因为这 $6$ 个和的总和等于原数列所有项之和 $108$，所以：
   $$6S_1 + 15d = 108 \quad \Rightarrow \quad 2S_1 + 5d = 36$$

3. **结合选项代入验证**
   由于 $S_1$ 是集合中最小的两个数之和，集合中最小的两个数为 $1$ 和 $3$，故必有 $S_1 \ge 1+3=4$。
   由 $2S_1 = 36 - 5d$ 可知，$36-5d$ 必须为正偶数。逐项验证选项：
   - **若 $d=2$**：$2S_1 = 36 - 10 = 26 \Rightarrow S_1 = 13$。此时和数列为 $13, 15, 17, 19, 21, 23$。最大和为 $23$，需用集合中的两个数相加得到。集合最大数为 $19$，需配对 $4$，但集合中无 $4$；次大 $17$ 需配对 $6$，亦无。故 $d=2$ 不成立。
   - **若 $d=4$**：$2S_1 = 36 - 20 = 16 \Rightarrow S_1 = 8$。此时和数列为 $8, 12, 16, 20, 24, 28$。尝试配对：
     $19+9=28$，$17+7=24$，$15+5=20$，$13+3=16$，$11+1=12$，$5+3=8$。
     所有数恰好各用一次，完全符合题意。故 $d=4$ 成立。
   - **若 $d=6$**：$2S_1 = 36 - 30 = 6 \Rightarrow S_1 = 3$。但最小两数之和为 $4$，不可能为 $3$，舍去。
   - **若 $d=8$**：$2S_1 = 36 - 40 = -4$，和不能为负，舍去。

4. **结论**
   满足条件的公差 $d$ 唯一确定为 $4$，对应选项 B。

Prompt
请解答以下数学题（单选题）。逐步推理，将最终答案放在 \boxed{} 中。

8. 设U={(x_1, x_2, x_3)|x_i \in \{-2,-1,1,2"}, i=1,2,3}\}为空间中64个点构成的集合，点P(1,1,1)，记样本空间\Omega = C_U P，从\Omega中随机取一个点，定义随机变量X如下：对\Omega中的每个点A(x_1, x_2, x_3)，令X(A) = x_1 + x_2 + x_3，则X的数学期望值为（ ）
A. -\frac{1}{21}
B. -\frac{1}{63}
C. 0
D. \frac{1}{7}

请将答案放在 \boxed{} 中。
Response
**逐步推理：**

1. **理解集合与样本空间**
   - 集合 $U$ 中的点坐标 $x_1, x_2, x_3$ 均独立取自 $\{-2, -1, 1, 2\}$，故 $|U| = 4^3 = 64$。
   - 点 $P(1,1,1) \in U$。
   - 符号 $C_U P$ 表示 $P$ 在 $U$ 中的补集，即样本空间 $\Omega = U \setminus \{P\}$，包含 $64 - 1 = 63$ 个点。
   - 从 $\Omega$ 中随机取一个点，每个点被取到的概率均为 $\frac{1}{63}$。

2. **利用对称性计算 $U$ 中所有点的 $X$ 值之和**
   - 对任意 $A(x_1, x_2, x_3) \in U$，$X(A) = x_1 + x_2 + x_3$。
   - 考虑 $U$ 中所有点的 $x_1$ 之和：每个 $x_1$ 取值都会与 $x_2, x_3$ 的 $4 \times 4 = 16$ 种组合配对。
     $$\sum_{A \in U} x_1 = 16 \times (-2 -1 + 1 + 2) = 16 \times 0 = 0$$
   - 同理，由对称性可知：
     $$\sum_{A \in U} x_2 = 0, \quad \sum_{A \in U} x_3 = 0$$
   - 因此，$U$ 中所有点的 $X$ 值总和为：
     $$\sum_{A \in U} X(A) = \sum_{A \in U} (x_1 + x_2 + x_3) = 0 + 0 + 0 = 0$$

3. **计算样本空间 $\Omega$ 中 $X$ 值的总和与期望**
   - $\Omega$ 是 $U$ 去掉点 $P$ 后的集合，故：
     $$\sum_{A \in \Omega} X(A) = \sum_{A \in U} X(A) - X(P)$$
   - 点 $P(1,1,1)$ 对应的 $X(P) = 1 + 1 + 1 = 3$。
   - 所以 $\sum_{A \in \Omega} X(A) = 0 - 3 = -3$。
   - 随机变量 $X$ 的数学期望为：
     $$E(X) = \frac{1}{63} \sum_{A \in \Omega} X(A) = \frac{-3}{63} = -\frac{1}{21}$$

4. **匹配选项**
   - 计算结果 $-\frac{1}{21}$ 对应选项 A。

最终答案为：
\boxed{A}
Reasoning
Here's a thinking process:

1.  **Analyze the User Input:**
   - **Set U:** $U = \{(x_1, x_2, x_3) \mid x_i \in \{-2, -1, 1, 2\}, i=1,2,3\}$. This means each coordinate can take 4 values. Total points in $U = 4^3 = 64$.
   - **Point P:** $P(1, 1, 1)$.
   - **Sample Space $\Omega$:** $\Omega = C_U P$. The notation $C_U P$ usually means the complement of $\{P\}$ in $U$, i.e., $\Omega = U \setminus \{P\}$. So $\Omega$ contains $64 - 1 = 63$ points.
   - **Random Variable X:** For each $A(x_1, x_2, x_3) \in \Omega$, $X(A) = x_1 + x_2 + x_3$.
   - **Goal:** Find the expected value $E[X]$.
   - **Options:** A. $-\frac{1}{21}$, B. $-\frac{1}{63}$, C. $0$, D. $\frac{1}{7}$.

2.  **Understand the Problem Mathematically:**
   - We have a uniform probability distribution over $\Omega$. Each point in $\Omega$ has probability $\frac{1}{63}$.
   - $E[X] = \frac{1}{63} \sum_{A \in \Omega} X(A) = \frac{1}{63} \sum_{A \in U \setminus \{P\}} (x_1 + x_2 + x_3)$.
   - Let $S_U = \sum_{A \in U} (x_1 + x_2 + x_3)$.
   - Then $\sum_{A \in \Omega} X(A) = S_U - X(P)$.
   - $X(P) = 1 + 1 + 1 = 3$.
   - So $E[X] = \frac{S_U - 3}{63}$.

3.  **Calculate $S_U$:**
   - $S_U = \sum_{x_1 \in \{-2,-1,1,2\}} \sum_{x_2 \in \{-2,-1,1,2\}} \sum_{x_3 \in \{-2,-1,1,2\}} (x_1 + x_2 + x_3)$.
   - By linearity of summation: $S_U = \sum_{x_1,x_2,x_3} x_1 + \sum_{x_1,x_2,x_3} x_2 + \sum_{x_1,x_2,x_3} x_3$.
   - Consider the first term: $\sum_{x_1,x_2,x_3} x_1 = \left(\sum_{x_1} x_1\right) \left(\sum_{x_2} 1\right) \left(\sum_{x_3} 1\right)$.
   - $\sum_{x_1 \in \{-2,-1,1,2\}} x_1 = -2 + (-1) + 1 + 2 = 0$.
   - So the first term is $0 \times 4 \times 4 = 0$.
   - Similarly, the second and third terms are also 0.
   - Therefore, $S_U = 0$.

   Wait, let me double check this. The set of values for each coordinate is symmetric around 0: $\{-2, -1, 1, 2\}$. The sum is indeed 0. So the sum of $x_1$ over all 64 points is 0. Same for $x_2$ and $x_3$. Thus, the total sum over $U$ of $x_1+x_2+x_3$ is 0.

4.  **Calculate $E[X]$:**
   - $S_U = 0$.
   - Sum over $\Omega$ is $S_U - X(P) = 0 - 3 = -3$.
   - $E[X] = \frac{-3}{63} = -\frac{1}{21}$.

5.  **Match with Options:**
   - A. $-\frac{1}{21}$
   - B. $-\frac{1}{63}$
   - C. $0$
   - D. $\frac{1}{7}$

gaokao_000_008 ✓ ACD ACD 2223 57.5 38.7 http://192.168.0.41:9292
Prompt
请解答以下数学题（多选题）。逐步推理，将最终答案放在 \boxed{} 中。

9. 设z = 3 + 2i，则（ ）
A. \bar{z} = 3 - 2i
B. |z| = 5
C. z^2 = 5 + 12i
D. \frac{z+3}{z-i} \in \mathbf{R}

请将答案放在 \boxed{} 中。
Response
我们逐项分析选项：

已知复数 $z = 3 + 2i$。

**A. $\bar{z} = 3 - 2i$**
复数的共轭定义：若 $z = a + bi$（$a,b\in\mathbf{R}$），则 $\bar{z} = a - bi$。
此处 $a=3,\ b=2$，故 $\bar{z} = 3 - 2i$。
✅ **A 正确**

**B. $|z| = 5$**
复数的模定义：$|z| = \sqrt{a^2 + b^2}$。
计算得 $|z| = \sqrt{3^2 + 2^2} = \sqrt{9 + 4} = \sqrt{13} \neq 5$。
❌ **B 错误**

**C. $z^2 = 5 + 12i$**
直接展开计算：
$$z^2 = (3 + 2i)^2 = 3^2 + 2 \cdot 3 \cdot 2i + (2i)^2 = 9 + 12i + 4i^2$$
因为 $i^2 = -1$，所以 $z^2 = 9 + 12i - 4 = 5 + 12i$。
✅ **C 正确**

**D. $\frac{z+3}{z-i} \in \mathbf{R}$**
将 $z = 3 + 2i$ 代入分式：
$$\frac{z+3}{z-i} = \frac{(3+2i)+3}{(3+2i)-i} = \frac{6+2i}{3+i}$$
观察分子可提取公因数 $2$：$6+2i = 2(3+i)$，因此
$$\frac{2(3+i)}{3+i} = 2 \quad (\text{分母 } 3+i \neq 0)$$
结果为实数 $2$，满足 $\in \mathbf{R}$。
✅ **D 正确**

综上所述，正确的选项为 A、C、D。

\boxed{ACD}

gaokao_000_009 ✓ BC BC 31700 48.7 650.8 http://192.168.0.41:9292
Prompt
请解答以下数学题（多选题）。逐步推理，将最终答案放在 \boxed{} 中。

10. 在空间中，A、B为两个定点，动点C到直线AB的距离为2，动点D到直线AB的距离为1。若二面角C - AB - D为60°，则（ ）
A. \angle CAD \ge 60^\circ
B. CD \ge \sqrt{3}
C. 当AB \perp CD时，CD \perp平面ABD
D. 当AB \perp平面ACD时，AC \perp AD

请将答案放在 \boxed{} 中。
Response
为了系统地解答此题，我们首先建立空间直角坐标系。

### 1. 建立坐标系与参数化
设直线 $AB$ 为 $z$ 轴，点 $A$ 为原点 $(0, 0, 0)$，点 $B$ 为 $(0, 0, b)$（$b \neq 0$）。
*   **动点 $C$：** 到直线 $AB$ 的距离为 2，设其 $z$ 坐标为 $z_C$。根据二面角定义，其投影在 $xy$ 平面上，坐标设为 $(2\cos 60^\circ, 2\sin 60^\circ) = (1, \sqrt{3})$。即 $C(1, \sqrt{3}, z_C)$。
*   **动点 $D$：** 到直线 $AB$ 的距离为 1，设其 $z$ 坐标为 $z_D$。其投影在 $xy$ 平面上，坐标设为 $(1, 0)$。即 $D(1, 0, z_D)$。
*   **验证二面角：** 平面 $ABC$ 法向量 $\vec{n_1} = (-\sqrt{3}, 1, 0)$，平面 $ABD$ 法向量 $\vec{n_2} = (0, 1, 0)$，$\cos \theta = \frac{\vec{n_1} \cdot \vec{n_2}}{|\vec{n_1}||\vec{n_2}|} = \frac{1}{2}$，符合 $60^\circ$ 的设定。

### 2. 逐步分析各选项

*   **选项 A：$\angle CAD \ge 60^\circ$**
    向量 $\vec{AC} = (1, \sqrt{3}, z_C)$，$\vec{AD} = (1, 0, z_D)$。
    $\cos \angle CAD = \frac{1 + z_C z_D}{\sqrt{4 + z_C^2} \cdot \sqrt{1 + z_D^2}}$。
    当 $z_C = z_D = k$ 且 $k \to \infty$ 时，$\cos \angle CAD \to 1$，即 $\angle CAD \to 0^\circ$。
    因此，$\angle CAD$ 可以小于 $60^\circ$。**选项 A 错误。**

*   **选项 B：$CD \ge \sqrt{3}$**
    计算 $CD$ 的距离平方：
    $|CD|^2 = (1-1)^2 + (\sqrt{3}-0)^2 + (z_C - z_D)^2 = 3 + (z_C - z_D)^2$。
    由于 $(z_C - z_D)^2 \ge 0$，则 $|CD|^2 \ge 3$，即 $CD \ge \sqrt{3}$。
    **选项 B 正确。**

*   **选项 C：当 $AB \perp CD$ 时，$CD \perp$ 平面 $ABD$**
    $AB$ 为 $z$ 轴，$AB \perp CD$ 意味着向量 $\vec{CD} = (0, -\sqrt{3}, z_D - z_C)$ 与 $(0, 0, 1)$ 垂直，即 $z_C = z_D$。
    此时 $\vec{CD} = (0, -\sqrt{3}, 0)$，该向量位于 $xy$ 平面内且垂直于 $x$ 轴。
    平面 $ABD$ 包含 $z$ 轴和点 $D(1, 0, z)$，其法向量为 $(0, 1, 0)$。
    由于 $\vec{CD}$ 平行于法向量 $(0, 1, 0)$，所以 $CD \perp$ 平面 $ABD$。
    **选项 C 正确。**

*   **选项 D：当 $AB \perp$ 平面 $ACD$ 时，$AC \perp AD$**
    若 $AB \perp$ 平面 $ACD$，则平面 $ACD$ 必须垂直于 $z$ 轴。由于 $A$ 在平面上且为原点，平面 $ACD$ 即为 $xy$ 平面（$z=0$）。
    此时 $C(1, \sqrt{3}, 0)$，$D(1, 0, 0)$。
    $\vec{AC} \cdot \vec{AD} = 1 \times 1 + \sqrt{3} \times 0 + 0 = 1 \neq 0$。
    此时 $\angle CAD = 60^\circ$，并非 $90^\circ$。
    **选项 D 错误。**

### 3. 结论
正确的选项是 B 和 C。

\boxed{BC}
Reasoning
Here's a thinking process to solve the problem:

1.  **Understand the Goal:** The objective is to evaluate four statements (A, B, C, D) given a geometric setup involving two fixed points $A, B$ and two moving points $C, D$ with specific distance constraints to the line $AB$ and a specific dihedral angle.

2.  **Analyze the Given Conditions:**
    *   **Fixed Points:** $A$ and $B$ define a line, let's call it $L_{AB}$.
    *   **Moving Point $C$:** The distance from $C$ to line $AB$ is 2. Let $P_C$ be the projection of $C$ onto line $AB$. Then $CP_C = 2$ and $CP_C \perp AB$.
    *   **Moving Point $D$:** The distance from $D$ to line $AB$ is 1. Let $P_D$ be the projection of $D$ onto line $AB$. Then $DP_D = 1$ and $DP_D \perp AB$.
    *   **Dihedral Angle:** The dihedral angle $C - AB - D$ is $60^\circ$. This means the angle between the plane containing $C$ and $AB$ and the plane containing $D$ and $AB$ is $60^\circ$. Since $CP_C \perp AB$ and $DP_D \perp AB$, the angle between the vectors $\vec{P_C C}$ and $\vec{P_D D}$ (or lines $CP_C$ and $DP_D$) depends on the relative positions of $P_C$ and $P_D$.
    *   *Correction on Dihedral Angle Definition:* The dihedral angle is defined by the angle between two rays starting from a point on the edge $AB$ and perpendicular to the edge. So, if $P_C$ and $P_D$ coincide, the angle $\angle C P_C D = 60^\circ$. If they don't coincide, the angle is still $60^\circ$ between the planes. This implies that if we project $C$ and $D$ onto a plane perpendicular to $AB$, the angle between the vectors from the origin to these projections is $60^\circ$.

    *   Dihedral angle condition: The angle between the vector $(x_C, y_C, 0)$ and $(x_D, y_D, 0)$ is $60^\circ$ or $120^\circ$? Usually, "dihedral angle" refers to the interior angle, often taken as the acute or specified angle. The problem states $60^\circ$. This means the angle between the projection vectors on the $xy$-plane is $60^\circ$ (or $-60^\circ$, which is the same cosine).
    *   Let's parameterize:
        *   $C$: Projection on $xy$-plane has length 2. Let's align $C$'s projection with the x-axis? No, relative to $D$.
        *   Let $C = (2\cos\theta_C, 2\sin\theta_C, z_C)$.
        *   Let $D = (1\cos\theta_D, 1\sin\theta_D, z_D)$.
        *   The angle between planes is $|\theta_C - \theta_D| = 60^\circ$ (modulo $360^\circ$). Let's assume $\theta_D = 0$ and $\theta_C = 60^\circ$ without loss of generality for distance calculations, but we must keep $z_C$ and $z_D$ as variables.
        *   So, $D = (1, 0, z_D)$.
        *   $C = (2\cos 60^\circ, 2\sin 60^\circ, z_C) = (1, \sqrt{3}, z_C)$.
        *   Wait, is the projection angle exactly $60^\circ$?
            *   Plane $CAB$ contains $z$-axis and $C$. Normal vector is perpendicular to $(1,0,0)$? No. The plane passing through $z$-axis and a point $(x,y,z)$ has normal vector perpendicular to $(x,y,0)$.
            *   Yes, the angle between the planes is the angle between their normals, which is the angle between the projections of $C$ and $D$ onto the $xy$-plane.

                *   $A = (0,0,0)$ (Since $A$ is on the line $AB$, and we can shift coordinates along $z$).

                *   Wait, is $A$ the origin? Yes, let's set $A = (0,0,0)$.
                *   $B$ is some point $(0,0,b)$.
                *   $C = (1, \sqrt{3}, z_C)$. Distance to $z$-axis is $\sqrt{1^2 + (\sqrt{3})^2} = 2$. Correct.
                *   $D = (1, 0, z_D)$. Distance to $z$-axis is $\sqrt{1^2 + 0} = 1$. Correct.
                *   Dihedral angle check: Vector $C$'s projection is $(1, \sqrt{3})$, angle $60^\circ$. Vector $D$'s projection is $(1, 0)$, angle $0^\circ$. Angle is $60^\circ$. Correct.
                *   $z_C$ and $z_D$ can be any real numbers.

4.  **Analyze Statement B: $CD \ge \sqrt{3}$**
    *   $C = (1, \sqrt{3}, z_C)$
    *   $D = (1, 0, z_D)$
    *   Distance squared $CD^2 = (1-1)^2 + (\sqrt{3}-0)^2 + (z_C - z_D)^2$
    *   $CD^2 = 0 + 3 + (z_C - z_D)^2 = 3 + (z_C - z_D)^2$
    *   Since $(z_C - z_D)^2 \ge 0$, $CD^2 \ge 3$.
    *   $CD \ge \sqrt{3}$.
    *   Equality holds when $z_C = z_D$.
    *   **Conclusion:** Statement B is **True**.

5.  **Analyze Statement A: $\angle CAD \ge 60^\circ$**
    *   $A = (0,0,0)$.
    *   $C = (1, \sqrt{3}, z_C)$.
    *   $D = (1, 0, z_D)$.
    *   Vectors:
        *   $\vec{AC} = (1, \sqrt{3}, z_C)$
        *   $\vec{AD} = (1, 0, z_D)$
    *   Dot product $\vec{AC} \cdot \vec{AD} = 1(1) + \sqrt{3}(0) + z_C z_D = 1 + z_C z_D$.
    *   Magnitudes:
        *   $|\vec{AC}| = \sqrt{1^2 + (\sqrt{3})^2 + z_C^2} = \sqrt{4 + z_C^2}$.
        *   $|\vec{AD}| = \sqrt{1^2 + 0^2 + z_D^2} = \sqrt{1 + z_D^2}$.
    *   $\cos(\angle CAD) = \frac{1 + z_C z_D}{\sqrt{4 + z_C^2}\sqrt{1 + z_D^2}}$.
    *   We need to check if $\angle CAD \ge 60^\circ$, which means $\cos(\angle CAD) \le \cos(60^\circ) = 1/2$.
    *   Is $\frac{1 + z_C z_D}{\sqrt{4 + z_C^2}\sqrt{1 + z_D^2}} \le \frac{1}{2}$ always true?
    *   Let's test a case. Let $z_C = 0, z_D = 0$.
        *   $C = (1, \sqrt{3}, 0)$, $D = (1, 0, 0)$.
        *   $\vec{AC} \cdot \vec{AD} = 1$.
        *   $|\vec{AC}| = 2$, $|\vec{AD}| = 1$.
        *   $\cos \theta = \frac{1}{2 \cdot 1} = \frac{1}{2}$.
        *   $\theta = 60^\circ$.
    *   Let's try to minimize the cosine (maximize angle). We need $1 + z_C z_D$ to be small (negative).
        *   Let $z_C = k, z_D = -k$ for large $k$.
        *   Numerator: $1 - k^2$.
        *   Denominator: $\sqrt{4+k^2}\sqrt{1+k^2} \approx k^2$.
        *   $\cos \theta \approx \frac{-k^2}{k^2} = -1$.
        *   $\theta \to 180^\circ$.
        *   So the angle can be larger than $60^\circ$.
    *   Let's try to maximize the cosine (minimize angle).
        *   We need $z_C, z_D$ to have same sign and be large.
        *   Let $z_C = k, z_D = k$ for large $k$.
        *   Numerator: $1 + k^2$.
        *   Denominator: $\sqrt{4+k^2}\sqrt{1+k^2} = \sqrt{4 + 5k^2 + k^4}$.
        *   $\cos \theta = \frac{1+k^2}{\sqrt{4 + 5k^2 + k^4}}$.
        *   Square it: $\frac{(1+k^2)^2}{4 + 5k^2 + k^4} = \frac{1 + 2k^2 + k^4}{4 + 5k^2 + k^4}$.
        *   As $k \to \infty$, ratio $\to 1$. $\theta \to 0^\circ$.
        *   Wait, is this possible?
        *   Let's visualize. $C$ is on cylinder radius 2, $D$ on radius 1. $A$ is on the axis.
        *   As $z$ gets very large, the points $C$ and $D$ look very close to the $z$-axis from $A$'s perspective?
        *   Actually, vectors $\vec{AC}$ and $\vec{AD}$ become almost parallel to the $z$-axis.
        *   Angle between them approaches 0.
        *   So $\angle CAD$ can be very small.
    *   Wait, did I set up the coordinates correctly regarding the "dihedral angle"?
        *   The dihedral angle is between plane $ABC$ and $ABD$.
        *   Plane $ABC$ contains $A(0,0,0)$, $B(0,0,1)$, $C(1, \sqrt{3}, z_C)$.
        *   Vector $\vec{AB} = (0,0,1)$. Vector $\vec{AC} = (1, \sqrt{3}, z_C)$.
        *   Normal $\vec{n_1} = \vec{AB} \times \vec{AC} = (0,0,1) \times (1, \sqrt{3}, z_C) = (-\sqrt{3}, 1, 0)$.
        *   Plane $ABD$ contains $A(0,0,0)$, $B(0,0,1)$, $D(1, 0, z_D)$.
        *   Vector $\vec{AD} = (1, 0, z_D)$.
        *   Normal $\vec{n_2} = \vec{AB} \times \vec{AD} = (0,0,1) \times (1, 0, z_D) = (0, 1, 0)$.
        *   Angle between normals: $\cos \alpha = \frac{\vec{n_1} \cdot \vec{n_2}}{|\vec{n_1}| |\vec{n_2}|} = \frac{1}{\sqrt{3+1} \cdot 1} = \frac{1}{2}$.
        *   Angle is $60^\circ$.
        *   My coordinate setup $C(1, \sqrt{3}, z_C)$ and $D(1, 0, z_D)$ is consistent with a $60^\circ$ dihedral angle.
    *   Back to $\angle CAD$.
        *   If $z_C, z_D \to \infty$ with same sign, $\vec{AC} \approx (0,0,1)$ and $\vec{AD} \approx (0,0,1)$. Angle $\to 0$.
        *   So $\angle CAD$ can be less than $60^\circ$.
        *   Wait, check the options again. A says $\angle CAD \ge 60^\circ$.
        *   My counter-example suggests it's false.
        *   Let's double check the geometry.
        *   $A$ is on the axis. $C$ is at distance 2 from axis. $D$ is at distance 1 from axis.
        *   Imagine looking down the axis from $A$. $C$ is at distance 2, $D$ at distance 1. Angle between their projections is $60^\circ$.
        *   The triangle $ACD$ projects onto the plane perpendicular to axis as a triangle with sides 2, 1 and angle $60^\circ$ at the origin.
        *   Wait, $A$ is the origin.
        *   In 3D, $C$ has height $z_C$, $D$ has height $z_D$.
        *   Let's check the case $z_C = z_D = 0$ again. $C$ is $(1, \sqrt{3}, 0)$, $D$ is $(1, 0, 0)$. $A$ is $(0,0,0)$.
        *   $AC = 2$, $AD = 1$, $CD = \sqrt{3}$.
        *   $2^2 + 1^2 = 5 \neq 3$.
        *   Cosine rule on $\triangle ACD$: $CD^2 = AC^2 + AD^2 - 2 AC \cdot AD \cos(\angle CAD)$.
        *   $3 = 4 + 1 - 2(2)(1) \cos(\angle CAD)$.
        *   $3 = 5 - 4 \cos(\angle CAD)$.
        *   $4 \cos(\angle CAD) = 2 \implies \cos(\angle CAD) = 1/2 \implies \angle CAD = 60^\circ$.
        *   So at $z_C=z_D=0$, angle is $60^\circ$.
        *   Now consider $z_C = 0, z_D$ large.
        *   $C = (1, \sqrt{3}, 0)$, $D = (1, 0, z_D)$.
        *   $AC = 2$.
        *   $AD = \sqrt{1 + z_D^2}$.
        *   $CD^2 = (1-1)^2 + (\sqrt{3}-0)^2 + (0-z_D)^2 = 3 + z_D^2$.
        *   Cosine rule: $3 + z_D^2 = 4 + (1 + z_D^2) - 2(2)(\sqrt{1+z_D^2}) \cos \theta$.
        *   $3 + z_D^2 = 5 + z_D^2 - 4\sqrt{1+z_D^2} \cos \theta$.
        *   $-2 = -4\sqrt{1+z_D^2} \cos \theta$.
        *   $\cos \theta = \frac{1}{2\sqrt{1+z_D^2}}$.
        *   As $z_D \to \infty$, $\cos \theta \to 0$, so $\theta \to 90^\circ$.
        *   Wait, if $\cos \theta$ is positive and small, angle is close to 90.
        *   What if $z_C, z_D$ are large and equal?
        *   $C = (1, \sqrt{3}, h)$, $D = (1, 0, h)$.
        *   $AC = \sqrt{4+h^2}$, $AD = \sqrt{1+h^2}$.
        *   $CD^2 = 3$.
        *   $3 = (4+h^2) + (1+h^2) - 2\sqrt{4+h^2}\sqrt{1+h^2} \cos \theta$.
        *   $3 = 5 + 2h^2 - 2\sqrt{4+h^2}\sqrt{1+h^2} \cos \theta$.
        *   $2\sqrt{4+h^2}\sqrt{1+h^2} \cos \theta = 2 + 2h^2$.
        *   $\cos \theta = \frac{1+h^2}{\sqrt{4+h^2}\sqrt{1+h^2}} = \sqrt{\frac{1+h^2}{4+h^2}}$.
        *   If $h=0$, $\cos \theta = \sqrt{1/4} = 1/2$, $\theta = 60^\circ$.
        *   If $h \to \infty$, $\cos \theta \to \sqrt{1} = 1$, $\theta \to 0^\circ$.
        *   Wait, if $\theta \to 0$, then $\angle CAD < 60^\circ$.
        *   So Statement A "$\angle CAD \ge 60^\circ$" is **False**.
    *   Let me re-read the problem carefully. Maybe I missed something.
        *   "A, B are two fixed points". "C distance to line AB is 2". "D distance to line AB is 1".
        *   "Dihedral angle C - AB - D is 60".
        *   Everything seems interpreted correctly.
        *   Let's check the logic of $\theta \to 0$.
        *   $A$ is at origin. Line $AB$ is $z$-axis.
        *   $C$ is at $(1, \sqrt{3}, h)$, distance to axis 2.
        *   $D$ is at $(1, 0, h)$, distance to axis 1.
        *   Vectors $\vec{AC}$ and $\vec{AD}$ both point "up" the $z$-axis.
        *   Angle between them is small.
        *   Is it possible for angle to be 0? No, because radial components are different, but angle approaches 0.
        *   So $\angle CAD$ can be less than 60.
        *   Therefore, A is false.

    *   Wait, maybe $A$ is not on the axis? "A, B are two fixed points". $C$ distance to *line* AB. Yes, A is on the line.
    *   Is it possible $C$ and $D$ are on the same side of the plane perpendicular to AB passing through A? Yes, $z_C, z_D$ can be anything.
    *   Is there a constraint I missed? "Dihedral angle C - AB - D".
        *   Usually, dihedral angles are defined by half-planes.
        *   Does the order of points matter? C-AB-D.
        *   It just defines the angle between the planes containing the segments AC and AD? No, planes containing lines AC and AB?
        *   Plane $CAB$ and Plane $DAB$. Yes.
        *   The angle between these planes is $60^\circ$.
        *   My setup preserves this.
    *   Let's check the case where $z_C$ and $z_D$ have opposite signs.
        *   $C = (1, \sqrt{3}, h)$, $D = (1, 0, -h)$.
        *   $\vec{AC} \cdot \vec{AD} = 1 + (-h^2) = 1 - h^2$.
        *   Lengths same as before.
        *   $\cos \theta = \frac{1-h^2}{\sqrt{4+h^2}\sqrt{1+h^2}}$.
        *   If $h$ is large, $\cos \theta \approx -1$. $\theta \approx 180^\circ$.
        *   If $h=0$, $\theta=60$.
        *   Is there a lower bound?
        *   Let's check if $\cos \theta$ can be greater than $1/2$.
        *   We found $\cos \theta = \sqrt{\frac{1+h^2}{4+h^2}}$ when $z_C=z_D=h$.
        *   Is $\sqrt{\frac{1+h^2}{4+h^2}} > \frac{1}{2}$?
        *   $\frac{1+h^2}{4+h^2} > \frac{1}{4} \iff 4(1+h^2) > 4+h^2 \iff 4 + 4h^2 > 4 + h^2 \iff 3h^2 > 0$.
        *   True for all $h \neq 0$.
        *   So $\cos \theta > 1/2$ for $h \neq 0$ (with same sign).
        *   This implies $\theta < 60^\circ$.
        *   So A is definitely false.

    *   **Wait**, let me re-read the problem statement *very* carefully. Maybe I swapped distances?
        *   "C distance to AB is 2". $r_C = 2$.
        *   "D distance to AB is 1". $r_D = 1$.
        *   $A=(0,0,0)$.
        *   $C = (2\cos\theta, 2\sin\theta, z_C)$.
        *   $D = (1\cos\phi, 1\sin\phi, z_D)$.
        *   Dihedral angle 60. $|\theta - \phi| = 60$ or $300$.
        *   Let $\theta = 60, \phi = 0$.
        *   $C = (1, \sqrt{3}, z_C)$, $D = (1, 0, z_D)$.
        *   Wait, $C$'s x-coord is $2 \cos 60 = 1$. $y$ is $2 \sin 60 = \sqrt{3}$.
        *   $D$'s x-coord is $1 \cos 0 = 1$. $y$ is 0.
        *   Wait, $D$ is $(1, 0, z_D)$. $C$ is $(1, \sqrt{3}, z_C)$.
        *   Distance $CD^2 = 0 + 3 + (z_C-z_D)^2 \ge 3$. Correct.
        *   Angle $CAD$.
        *   $A$ is origin.
        *   $\vec{AC} = (1, \sqrt{3}, z_C)$.
        *   $\vec{AD} = (1, 0, z_D)$.
        *   Dot product $1 + z_C z_D$.
        *   Lengths $\sqrt{4+z_C^2}, \sqrt{1+z_D^2}$.
        *   $\cos \angle CAD = \frac{1 + z_C z_D}{\sqrt{4+z_C^2}\sqrt{1+z_D^2}}$.
        *   If $z_C=z_D=k$, $\cos \alpha = \frac{1+k^2}{\sqrt{4+k^2}\sqrt{1+k^2}} = \sqrt{\frac{1+k^2}{4+k^2}}$.
        *   For $k \to \infty$, $\cos \alpha \to 1$, $\alpha \to 0$.
        *   So angle can be very small.
        *   Statement A: $\angle CAD \ge 60^\circ$ is FALSE.

    *   **Is there any constraint I am missing?**
        *   "A, B are fixed points". "C to line AB distance 2". "D to line AB distance 1".
        *   Maybe $C$ and $D$ are restricted to a specific segment? No, "moving points" (动点).
        *   Maybe "distance to line AB" means distance to the segment? No, usually "line" (直线) means infinite line. If it meant segment, it would say "segment AB".
        *   However, in some contexts (like high school math problems in China), "distance to line AB" is standard.
        *   Is it possible that $A$ is not the origin?
        *   Coordinate geometry allows shifting $A$ to origin without changing angles at $A$.
        *   Is it possible that $A$ is not on the line $AB$? No, $A$ and $B$ define the line. $A$ is a point on the line.

    *   Let's check the Chinese terminology just in case.
        *   "动点C到直线AB的距离" -> Moving point C's distance to line AB.
        *   "二面角C - AB - D" -> Dihedral angle formed by planes CAB and DAB.
        *   Everything seems standard.
        *   Why would A be an option? Maybe to catch people who think the projection angle is the angle in space?
        *   Projection of $\vec{AC}$ on xy plane makes 60 deg with projection of $\vec{AD}$.
        *   But the vectors themselves can be close to z-axis, making their angle small.
        *   Wait, if $\vec{AC}$ is close to $z$-axis, then $C$ is very far from $xy$-plane relative to distance to axis.
        *   $z_C \gg 2$.
        *   Yes, this is physically possible.
        *   So A is likely incorrect.

6.  **Analyze Statement C: When $AB \perp CD$, then $CD \perp$ Plane $ABD$**
    *   $AB$ is the $z$-axis, direction $(0,0,1)$.
    *   $C = (1, \sqrt{3}, z_C)$.
    *   $D = (1, 0, z_D)$.
    *   Vector $\vec{CD} = D - C = (0, -\sqrt{3}, z_D - z_C)$.
    *   Condition: $AB \perp CD$.
    *   Dot product $\vec{AB} \cdot \vec{CD} = (0,0,1) \cdot (0, -\sqrt{3}, z_D - z_C) = z_D - z_C = 0$.
    *   So $z_D = z_C$. Let's call it $z$.
    *   So $C = (1, \sqrt{3}, z)$, $D = (1, 0, z)$.
    *   Vector $\vec{CD} = (0, -\sqrt{3}, 0)$. This vector is in the $xy$-plane.
    *   We need to check if $CD \perp$ Plane $ABD$.
    *   Plane $ABD$ contains points $A(0,0,0)$, $B(0,0,b)$, $D(1, 0, z)$.
    *   Vectors in Plane $ABD$:
        *   $\vec{AB} = (0,0,1)$ (direction).
        *   $\vec{AD} = (1, 0, z)$.
    *   Normal to Plane $ABD$, $\vec{n} = \vec{AB} \times \vec{AD} = (0,0,1) \times (1, 0, z) = (0, 1, 0)$.
    *   Is $\vec{CD}$ parallel to $\vec{n}$?
    *   $\vec{CD} = (0, -\sqrt{3}, 0)$.
    *   $\vec{n} = (0, 1, 0)$.
    *   Yes, they are parallel (proportional).
    *   Therefore, $CD$ is perpendicular to the Plane $ABD$.
    *   **Conclusion:** Statement C is **True**.

7.  **Analyze Statement D: When $AB \perp$ Plane $ACD$, then $AC \perp AD$**
    *   Condition: $AB \perp$ Plane $ACD$.
    *   $AB$ is the $z$-axis.
    *   So Plane $ACD$ must be perpendicular to the $z$-axis.
    *   This means Plane $ACD$ is a horizontal plane $z = constant$.
    *   Since $A(0,0,0)$ is in Plane $ACD$, the plane must be $z=0$.
    *   So $C$ and $D$ must have $z$-coordinate 0.
    *   $z_C = 0, z_D = 0$.
    *   Coordinates:
        *   $A = (0,0,0)$.
        *   $C = (1, \sqrt{3}, 0)$.
        *   $D = (1, 0, 0)$.
    *   Check if $AC \perp AD$.
    *   $\vec{AC} = (1, \sqrt{3}, 0)$.
    *   $\vec{AD} = (1, 0, 0)$.
    *   Dot product $\vec{AC} \cdot \vec{AD} = 1 \cdot 1 + \sqrt{3} \cdot 0 + 0 = 1$.
    *   Dot product is not 0.
    *   So $AC$ is not perpendicular to $AD$.
    *   Angle is $60^\circ$ (as calculated before).
    *   **Conclusion:** Statement D is **False**.

8.  **Re-evaluating Statement A (Double Check)**
    *   Maybe I calculated $C$ and $D$ coordinates wrong?

    *   Wait, if $C$ and $D$ are on the same side of the plane perpendicular to $AB$ at $A$, does it change anything?

    *   Let $z_C = z_D = z$.
    *   $f(z) = \frac{1+z^2}{\sqrt{4+z^2}\sqrt{1+z^2}} = \sqrt{\frac{(1+z^2)^2}{(4+z^2)(1+z^2)}} = \sqrt{\frac{1+z^2}{4+z^2}}$.
    *   At $z=0$, value is $\sqrt{1/4} = 1/2$.
    *   As $z \to \infty$, value $\to 1$.

    *   Why would it be 0?
    *   $C$ is far up, $D$ is far up. Both close to the line $AB$. $A$ is at origin.
    *   $C$ is at distance 2 from line, height $H$. $D$ is distance 1 from line, height $H$.
    *   Triangle $ACD$. $AC \approx H$, $AD \approx H$, $CD = \sqrt{3}$ (horizontal distance).
    *   By cosine rule: $3 = H^2 + H^2 - 2H^2 \cos \theta = 2H^2(1-\cos \theta)$.
    *   $1 - \cos \theta = \frac{3}{2H^2}$.
    *   $\cos \theta = 1 - \frac{3}{2H^2}$.
    *   For large $H$, $\cos \theta \approx 1$, $\theta \approx 0$.
    *   This seems physically correct.
    *   So A is definitely False.

    *   Usually these are "Select all correct options" (多选题).
    *   So A might be false.
    *   Let's re-verify B, C, D.

    *   **Re-verify B:** $CD \ge \sqrt{3}$.
        *   $C = (1, \sqrt{3}, z_C)$, $D = (1, 0, z_D)$.
        *   $CD^2 = 0 + 3 + (z_C-z_D)^2 \ge 3$.
        *   True.
        *   Wait, is this coordinate setup general?
        *   $C$ distance 2, $D$ distance 1, angle 60.
        *   In the plane perpendicular to $AB$, let $O$ be the projection of any point on $AB$.
        *   $C'$ is proj of $C$, $D'$ is proj of $D$.
        *   $OC' = 2, OD' = 1, \angle C'OD' = 60^\circ$.
        *   Distance $C'D'$ in that plane:
        *   $C'D'^2 = 2^2 + 1^2 - 2(2)(1)\cos 60 = 4 + 1 - 2 = 3$.
        *   $C'D' = \sqrt{3}$.
        *   $C$ and $D$ differ by a vertical component $z_C - z_D$ along $AB$.
        *   $CD^2 = C'D'^2 + (z_C - z_D)^2 = 3 + (z_C - z_D)^2 \ge 3$.
        *   $CD \ge \sqrt{3}$.
        *   Yes, B is solid.

    *   **Re-verify C:** When $AB \perp CD$, then $CD \perp$ Plane $ABD$.
        *   $AB$ is vertical.
        *   $CD \perp AB$ means $z_C = z_D$.
        *   Then $C$ and $D$ are at same height.
        *   $C'$ and $D'$ are their projections.
        *   Vector $\vec{CD}$ is horizontal, parallel to $\vec{C'D'}$.
        *   $\vec{C'D'}$ is in the plane perpendicular to $AB$.
        *   Plane $ABD$ contains the line $AB$ (vertical) and point $D$.
        *   Since $D$ projects to $D'$, the plane $ABD$ is the plane passing through $AB$ and $D'$.
        *   Wait, the plane containing line $AB$ and point $D$ is a vertical plane.
        *   Is the vector $\vec{CD}$ (which is $\vec{C'D'}$) perpendicular to this vertical plane?
        *   The normal to Plane $ABD$ is a horizontal vector perpendicular to $OD'$.
        *   $O$ is on $AB$ (say origin). $D'$ is $(1,0)$. $AB$ is $z$-axis.
        *   Plane $ABD$ contains $z$-axis and $(1,0,z_D)$.
        *   Normal is $(0,1,0)$ (pointing in y direction).
        *   Vector $\vec{CD} = D - C = (1, 0, z) - (1, \sqrt{3}, z) = (0, -\sqrt{3}, 0)$.
        *   This vector is parallel to $(0,1,0)$.
        *   So $\vec{CD}$ is perpendicular to Plane $ABD$.
        *   Yes, C is solid.
        *   Wait, does $AB \perp CD$ imply $C$ and $D$ are at same height?
        *   $AB$ is $z$-axis. $CD$ vector $(x_D-x_C, y_D-y_C, z_D-z_C)$.
        *   Dot product with $(0,0,1)$ is $z_D-z_C$.
        *   So yes, $z_C = z_D$.
        *   So C is True.

    *   **Re-verify D:** When $AB \perp$ Plane $ACD$, then $AC \perp AD$.
        *   $AB$ is $z$-axis.
        *   Plane $ACD \perp z$-axis $\implies$ Plane $ACD$ is horizontal ($z=const$).
        *   Since $A$ is in plane $ACD$ and $A$ is on $z$-axis (origin), $z=0$.
        *   So $C, D$ must be in $xy$-plane.
        *   $C = (1, \sqrt{3}, 0)$, $D = (1, 0, 0)$.
        *   $A = (0,0,0)$.
        *   $\vec{AC} = (1, \sqrt{3}, 0)$.
        *   $\vec{AD} = (1, 0, 0)$.
        *   Dot product = 1. Not 0.
        *   So $AC$ not perp to $AD$.
        *   D is False.

    *   **Re-verify A:** $\angle CAD \ge 60^\circ$.
        *   We found it can be close to 0.
        *   Wait, is there a constraint "A, B fixed"?
        *   Maybe $C$ and $D$ cannot be arbitrarily far?
        *   No constraint.
        *   Maybe I should check the case where angle is minimized?
        *   We found minimum angle is 0?
        *   Let's check the wording of A again.
        *   Maybe it's $\angle CAD \le 60^\circ$? No, text says $\ge$.
        *   Maybe I made a mistake in the calculation of $\cos \theta$?
        *   $\vec{AC} \cdot \vec{AD} = 1 + z_C z_D$.
        *   $|\vec{AC}|^2 = 4 + z_C^2$.
        *   $|\vec{AD}|^2 = 1 + z_D^2$.
        *   $\cos \theta = \frac{1 + z_C z_D}{\sqrt{4+z_C^2}\sqrt{1+z_D^2}}$.
        *   Let's check if this can be $> 1/2$.
        *   We want $\frac{1 + z_C z_D}{\sqrt{4+z_C^2}\sqrt{1+z_D^2}} > \frac{1}{2}$.
        *   Square both sides (assuming numerator positive, if negative angle > 90 > 60, so inequality holds).
        *   Assume $z_C, z_D > 0$.
        *   $4(1 + z_C z_D)^2 > (4+z_C^2)(1+z_D^2)$.
        *   $4(1 + 2z_C z_D + z_C^2 z_D^2) > 4 + 4z_D^2 + z_C^2 + z_C^2 z_D^2$.
        *   $4 + 8z_C z_D + 4z_C^2 z_D^2 > 4 + 4z_D^2 + z_C^2 + z_C^2 z_D^2$.
        *   $8z_C z_D + 3z_C^2 z_D^2 > 4z_D^2 + z_C^2$.
        *   Let $z_C = z_D = k$.
        *   $8k^2 + 3k^4 > 4k^2 + k^2 = 5k^2$.
        *   $3k^4 + 3k^2 > 0$.
        *   True for all $k \neq 0$.
        *   So $\cos \theta > 1/2$ for any non-zero $k$ (same sign).
        *   $\theta < 60^\circ$.
        *   So A is False.

    *   **Is there any interpretation where A is true?**
        *   Maybe "Distance to line AB" means distance to the *segment*?
        *   If $C$ must be within the projection of the segment?
        *   No, "line" usually means infinite line.
        *   Maybe "A, B" are distinct, so length AB > 0.
        *   Doesn't change anything.
        *   Maybe the dihedral angle definition is different?
        *   "C - AB - D".
        *   Standard definition: angle between plane ABC and ABD.
        *   Correct.
        *   Maybe I calculated $CD$ wrong?
        *   $C=(1, \sqrt{3}, z_C)$, $D=(1, 0, z_D)$.
        *   Wait, distance of $C$ from origin in xy plane is 2.
        *   $1^2 + (\sqrt{3})^2 = 4$. Correct.
        *   Distance of $D$ from origin in xy plane is 1.
        *   $1^2 + 0 = 1$. Correct.
        *   Angle between vectors $(1, \sqrt{3})$ and $(1, 0)$?
        *   Dot product $1*1 + \sqrt{3}*0 = 1$.
        *   Norms 2 and 1.
        *   $\cos = 1/2$. Angle 60. Correct.
        *   Wait, $D=(1,0,z_D)$. $C=(1, \sqrt{3}, z_C)$.
        *   Vector $\vec{CD} = (0, -\sqrt{3}, z_D-z_C)$.
        *   Length squared $3 + \Delta z^2$.
        *   This seems robust.

    *   **Let's check if there is an alternative configuration.**
        *   The angle between planes is 60.
        *   Vectors $(x_C, y_C)$ and $(x_D, y_D)$ have angle 60 or 120?
        *   Dihedral angle is usually in $[0, 180]$. 60 is given.
        *   If the angle between normals was 120, cosine would be -1/2.
        *   Angle between vectors in plane is same as angle between normals?
        *   Normal $\vec{n_1} \perp$ Plane 1. Normal $\vec{n_2} \perp$ Plane 2.
        *   Angle between planes is angle between normals (or 180 minus that).
        *   If angle is 60, then vectors in plane perpendicular to intersection line make angle 60.
        *   Yes.
        *   So my setup is correct.

    *   **Could Statement A be $\angle CAD \le 60^\circ$?**
        *   No, text is fixed.
    *   **Could Statement A be correct under some other interpretation?**

        *   Maybe "distance to line AB" means distance from $C$ to $B$? No.

    *   **Let's pause and think if there's a geometric reason why $\angle CAD$ should be $\ge 60$.**
        *   Consider the cone of points with distance 2 from $AB$. No, cylinder.
        *   $C$ is on cylinder radius 2. $D$ is on cylinder radius 1.
        *   Angle of $C$ around axis is 60 relative to $D$.
        *   $A$ is on axis.
        *   Look at triangle $ACD$.
        *   $AC = \sqrt{r_C^2 + z_C^2} = \sqrt{4 + z_C^2}$.
        *   $AD = \sqrt{r_D^2 + z_D^2} = \sqrt{1 + z_D^2}$.
        *   $CD = \sqrt{r_C^2 + r_D^2 - 2r_C r_D \cos 60 + (z_C-z_D)^2} = \sqrt{4 + 1 - 2 + (z_C-z_D)^2} = \sqrt{3 + (z_C-z_D)^2}$.
        *   We want to bound angle $\alpha = \angle CAD$.
        *   Cosine rule: $CD^2 = AC^2 + AD^2 - 2 AC \cdot AD \cos \alpha$.
        *   $3 + (z_C-z_D)^2 = (4+z_C^2) + (1+z_D^2) - 2 AC \cdot AD \cos \alpha$.
        *   $3 + z_C^2 - 2z_C z_D + z_D^2 = 5 + z_C^2 + z_D^2 - 2 AC \cdot AD \cos \alpha$.
        *   $3 - 2z_C z_D = 5 - 2 AC \cdot AD \cos \alpha$.
        *   $2 AC \cdot AD \cos \alpha = 5 - 3 + 2z_C z_D = 2 + 2z_C z_D$.
        *   $AC \cdot AD \cos \alpha = 1 + z_C z_D$.
        *   $\cos \alpha = \frac{1 + z_C z_D}{\sqrt{4+z_C^2}\sqrt{1+z_D^2}}$.
        *   This matches my previous derivation.
        *   We established that if $z_C, z_D$ are large and positive, $\cos \alpha \to 1$, so $\alpha \to 0$.
        *   Wait, is it possible that for large $z$, the points are "close" to the line $AB$?
        *   Yes, angularly from $A$, they look close.
        *   Imagine $A$ is eye. Line $AB$ is a pole.
        *   $C$ is a point on a wire wrapped around pole radius 2.
        *   $D$ is on a wire radius 1.
        *   Angular separation 60 deg.
        *   If both are very high up (large $z$), they appear close to the vertical line of the pole.
        *   The angle between them is small.
        *   So A is false.

    *   **Let's check Statement B again.**

    *   **Wait, let me double check the "Dihedral Angle" definition.**
        *   Is it possible the dihedral angle is defined such that the angle between projections is not 60?

        *   If planes are $y=0$ (containing $D$) and rotated by 60.
        *   Normal 1: $(0,1,0)$.

        *   Wait, if plane passes through z-axis, normal is in xy plane.
        *   Angle between normals is 60.
        *   Angle between planes is 60.
        *   The intersection of plane 1 ($y=0$) with $z=0$ plane is x-axis.
        *   Intersection of plane 2 with $z=0$ plane is line at 60 deg to x-axis?
        *   Let's check.
        *   Plane 2 normal is $(-\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}, \frac{1}{2}, 0)$.
        *   Equation: $-\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}x + \frac{1}{2}y = 0 \implies y = \sqrt{3}x$.
        *   Angle of line $y=\sqrt{3}x$ is 60 deg.
        *   So points on plane 2 have $y = \sqrt{3}x$.

        *   So $C$ must lie on a plane at 60 deg to $y=0$.
        *   So $C$'s projection on xy plane must be on a line at 60 deg to x-axis (or -60).
        *   My coordinates: $C = (1, \sqrt{3}, z_C)$. $y/x = \sqrt{3}/1 = \sqrt{3}$. Angle 60.
        *   $D = (1, 0, z_D)$. $y/x = 0$. Angle 0.

    *   **Let's check if there's a calculation error in $\cos \theta$ for Statement A.**
        *   $\cos \theta = \frac{1 + z_C z_D}{\sqrt{4+z_C^2}\sqrt{1+z_D^2}}$.
        *   Let $z_C = z_D = 10$.

    *   **Let's check Statement C logic again.**

        *   $\begin{vmatrix} i & j & k \\ 0 & 0 & 1 \\ 1 & 0 & z \end{vmatrix} = i(0) - j(-1) + k(0) = (0, 1, 0)$.
        *   Normal is $(0,1,0)$.

        *   Yes, $(0, -\sqrt{3}, 0) = -\sqrt{3} (0, 1, 0)$.

        *   To check if line is perp to plane, check if direction is parallel to normal.
        *   Or check if direction is perp to *every* vector in the plane.

    *   **Let's check option C again.**

        *   Is it possible that $CD \perp$ Plane $ABD$ is not true?

gaokao_000_010 ✓ BCD BCD 12324 52.7 233.7 http://192.168.0.41:9292
Prompt
请解答以下数学题（多选题）。逐步推理，将最终答案放在 \boxed{} 中。

11. 已知圆C_1:(x+1)^2+y^2=1，圆C_2:(x-1)^2+y^2=1，圆C_3:x^2+(y-\sqrt{3})^2=1，直线l:y=kx+b与C_1, C_2, C_3均有两个交点。记l被C_1, C_2, C_3截得的弦长分别为s_1、s_2、s_3，则（ ）
A. k可以取任意实数
B. 满足s_1=s_2=s_3的直线l共有3条
C. 满足s_1+s_2+s_3=3的直线l多于3条
D. 当b=0时，s_1+s_2+s_3的最大值为\frac{2\sqrt{21}}{3}

请将答案放在 \boxed{} 中。
Response
**逐步推理如下：**

设直线 $l: kx - y + b = 0$ 到圆 $C_1, C_2, C_3$ 的圆心 $O_1(-1,0), O_2(1,0), O_3(0,\sqrt{3})$ 的距离分别为 $d_1, d_2, d_3$。
由于三圆半径均为 $r=1$，弦长公式为 $s_i = 2\sqrt{1-d_i^2}$。直线与圆有两个交点的充要条件是 $0 \le d_i < 1$。
由点到直线距离公式得：
$d_1 = \frac{|b-k|}{\sqrt{k^2+1}},\quad d_2 = \frac{|b+k|}{\sqrt{k^2+1}},\quad d_3 = \frac{|b-\sqrt{3}|}{\sqrt{k^2+1}}$。

**分析选项 A：**
直线与三圆均有两个交点，需同时满足 $d_1<1, d_2<1, d_3<1$。
由 $d_1<1$ 且 $d_2<1$ 得 $(b-k)^2 < k^2+1$ 且 $(b+k)^2 < k^2+1$，两式相加得 $2b^2 < 2 \Rightarrow |b|<1$。
进一步可得 $|b| < \sqrt{k^2+1}-|k|$。
由 $d_3<1$ 得 $|b-\sqrt{3}| < \sqrt{k^2+1}$。因 $|b|<1<\sqrt{3}$，故 $b-\sqrt{3}<0$，不等式化为 $\sqrt{3}-b < \sqrt{k^2+1} \Rightarrow b > \sqrt{3}-\sqrt{k^2+1}$。
要使满足条件的 $b$ 存在，需区间 $(\sqrt{3}-\sqrt{k^2+1}, \sqrt{k^2+1}-|k|)$ 非空，即：
$\sqrt{3}-\sqrt{k^2+1} < \sqrt{k^2+1}-|k| \Rightarrow \sqrt{3}+|k| < 2\sqrt{k^2+1}$。
两边平方整理得 $(\sqrt{3}|k|-1)^2 > 0$，即 $|k| \ne \frac{\sqrt{3}}{3}$。
故 $k$ 不能取 $\pm \frac{\sqrt{3}}{3}$，**选项 A 错误**。

**分析选项 B：**
$s_1=s_2=s_3 \Leftrightarrow d_1=d_2=d_3$（因 $s=2\sqrt{1-d^2}$ 在 $[0,1)$ 上单调递减）。
由 $d_1=d_2 \Rightarrow |b-k|=|b+k| \Rightarrow b=0$ 或 $k=0$。
① 若 $b=0$，则 $d_1=d_2=\frac{|k|}{\sqrt{k^2+1}}$，$d_3=\frac{\sqrt{3}}{\sqrt{k^2+1}}$。令 $d_1=d_3$ 得 $|k|=\sqrt{3}$，即 $k=\pm\sqrt{3}$。对应直线 $y=\pm\sqrt{3}x$（均满足 $d_i<1$）。
② 若 $k=0$，则 $d_1=d_2=|b|$，$d_3=|b-\sqrt{3}|$。令 $d_1=d_3$ 得 $|b|=|b-\sqrt{3}| \Rightarrow b=\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}$。对应直线 $y=\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}$（满足 $d_i<1$）。
综上，共有 3 条直线，**选项 B 正确**。

**分析选项 C：**
考虑 $b=0$ 的情形，此时 $s_1=s_2=\frac{2}{\sqrt{k^2+1}}$，$s_3=2\sqrt{1-\frac{3}{k^2+1}}$（需 $k^2>2$）。
令 $s_1+s_2+s_3=3$，设 $u=\sqrt{k^2+1}>(\sqrt{3})$，方程化为 $\frac{4}{u}+2\sqrt{1-\frac{3}{u^2}}=3$。
整理得 $5u^2-24u+28=0$，解得 $u=2$ 或 $u=\frac{14}{5}$。
对应 $k^2=3$ 或 $k^2=\frac{171}{25}$，均满足 $k^2>2$。故仅 $b=0$ 时就有 4 条不同的直线满足条件，远多于 3 条。**选项 C 正确**。

**分析选项 D：**
当 $b=0$ 时，由 C 的分析知 $S = s_1+s_2+s_3 = \frac{4}{\sqrt{k^2+1}} + 2\sqrt{1-\frac{3}{k^2+1}}$。
令 $t=\frac{1}{\sqrt{k^2+1}}$，由 $k^2>2$ 得 $t \in (0, \frac{1}{\sqrt{3}})$。
$S(t) = 4t + 2\sqrt{1-3t^2}$。求导 $S'(t) = 4 - \frac{6t}{\sqrt{1-3t^2}}$。
令 $S'(t)=0$ 得 $2\sqrt{1-3t^2}=3t \Rightarrow 21t^2=4 \Rightarrow t=\frac{2}{\sqrt{21}}$（在定义域内）。
此时 $S_{\max} = \frac{8}{\sqrt{21}} + 2\sqrt{1-\frac{12}{21}} = \frac{8}{\sqrt{21}} + \frac{6}{\sqrt{21}} = \frac{14}{\sqrt{21}} = \frac{2\sqrt{21}}{3}$。
**选项 D 正确**。

综上所述，正确选项为 B、C、D。

\boxed{BCD}
Reasoning
Here's a thinking process:

1.  **Understand the User Input:**
    - **Problem Type:** Multiple-choice question (possibly multiple correct options).
    - **Given:**
      - Circle $C_1: (x+1)^2 + y^2 = 1$, center $O_1(-1, 0)$, radius $r_1 = 1$.
      - Circle $C_2: (x-1)^2 + y^2 = 1$, center $O_2(1, 0)$, radius $r_2 = 1$.
      - Circle $C_3: x^2 + (y-\sqrt{3})^2 = 1$, center $O_3(0, \sqrt{3})$, radius $r_3 = 1$.
      - Line $l: y = kx + b$ intersects all three circles at two distinct points.
      - Chord lengths intercepted by $l$ on $C_1, C_2, C_3$ are $s_1, s_2, s_3$ respectively.
    - **Options:**
      - A. $k$ can be any real number.
      - B. There are exactly 3 lines $l$ such that $s_1 = s_2 = s_3$.
      - C. There are more than 3 lines $l$ such that $s_1 + s_2 + s_3 = 3$.
      - D. When $b=0$, the maximum value of $s_1 + s_2 + s_3$ is $\frac{2\sqrt{21}}{3}$.
    - **Task:** Analyze each option, provide step-by-step reasoning, and output the final answer in `\boxed{}`.

2.  **Analyze the Geometry & Chord Length Formula:**
    - For a circle with radius $r$ and distance $d$ from its center to line $l$, the chord length is $s = 2\sqrt{r^2 - d^2}$.
    - Here, all radii are $r=1$. So $s_i = 2\sqrt{1 - d_i^2}$, where $d_i$ is the distance from $O_i$ to line $l$.
    - The condition "intersects at two points" means $0 \le d_i < 1$ for $i=1,2,3$.
    - Line $l: kx - y + b = 0$.
    - Distances:
      - $d_1 = \frac{|k(-1) - 0 + b|}{\sqrt{k^2+1}} = \frac{|b-k|}{\sqrt{k^2+1}}$
      - $d_2 = \frac{|k(1) - 0 + b|}{\sqrt{k^2+1}} = \frac{|b+k|}{\sqrt{k^2+1}}$
      - $d_3 = \frac{|k(0) - \sqrt{3} + b|}{\sqrt{k^2+1}} = \frac{|b-\sqrt{3}|}{\sqrt{k^2+1}}$
    - Chord lengths: $s_i = 2\sqrt{1 - d_i^2}$.

3.  **Evaluate Option A: $k$ can be any real number.**
    - For $l$ to intersect all three circles, we need $d_1 < 1, d_2 < 1, d_3 < 1$.
    - This implies $\frac{|b-k|}{\sqrt{k^2+1}} < 1$, $\frac{|b+k|}{\sqrt{k^2+1}} < 1$, $\frac{|b-\sqrt{3}|}{\sqrt{k^2+1}} < 1$.
    - Squaring: $(b-k)^2 < k^2+1 \Rightarrow b^2 - 2bk < 1$, $(b+k)^2 < k^2+1 \Rightarrow b^2 + 2bk < 1$, $(b-\sqrt{3})^2 < k^2+1$.
    - Adding the first two: $2b^2 < 2 \Rightarrow b^2 < 1 \Rightarrow |b| < 1$.
    - From $b^2 \pm 2bk < 1$, we need $|2bk| < 1 - b^2$. Since $|b|<1$, $1-b^2 > 0$, so $|k| < \frac{1-b^2}{2|b|}$ if $b \ne 0$. If $b=0$, $0 < 1$ holds for all $k$.
    - Also need $(b-\sqrt{3})^2 < k^2+1 \Rightarrow k^2 > (b-\sqrt{3})^2 - 1 = b^2 - 2\sqrt{3}b + 2$.
    - So for a given $b$, $k$ is constrained. Can $k$ be *any* real number regardless of $b$? The statement says "$k$ can take any real number", which usually means for some $b$, or in general? It likely means the set of possible $k$ for valid lines is $\mathbb{R}$.
    - Let's check if for any $k \in \mathbb{R}$, there exists a $b$ such that the line intersects all three circles.
    - We need to find $b$ satisfying:
      1. $|b| < 1$
      2. $|b| < \frac{1+k^2}{2|k|}$ (from $b^2 \pm 2bk < 1$)
      3. $k^2+1 > (b-\sqrt{3})^2 \Rightarrow |b-\sqrt{3}| < \sqrt{k^2+1}$.
    - Actually, let's fix $k$ and see if we can find $b$. The conditions on $b$ are:
      - $b \in (-\sqrt{k^2+1}+k, \sqrt{k^2+1}+k)$ from $d_1 < 1$.
      - $b \in (-\sqrt{k^2+1}-k, \sqrt{k^2+1}-k)$ from $d_2 < 1$.
      - Intersection of these two: $b \in (-\sqrt{k^2+1}+|k|, \sqrt{k^2+1}-|k|)$. Note that $\sqrt{k^2+1} > |k|$, so this interval is non-empty and symmetric around 0. Its length is $2(\sqrt{k^2+1}-|k|)$.
      - Also $|b| < 1$ is implied if $\sqrt{k^2+1}-|k| < 1$? Actually $\sqrt{k^2+1}-|k| = \frac{1}{\sqrt{k^2+1}+|k|} < 1$. So the intersection automatically satisfies $|b| < 1$.
      - Third condition: $|b-\sqrt{3}| < \sqrt{k^2+1} \Rightarrow b \in (\sqrt{3}-\sqrt{k^2+1}, \sqrt{3}+\sqrt{k^2+1})$.
    - We need the intersection of $I_1 = (-\sqrt{k^2+1}+|k|, \sqrt{k^2+1}-|k|)$ and $I_3 = (\sqrt{3}-\sqrt{k^2+1}, \sqrt{3}+\sqrt{k^2+1})$ to be non-empty.
    - Lower bound of $I_1$ is negative, upper bound is positive but small ($<1$).
    - Lower bound of $I_3$ is $\sqrt{3}-\sqrt{k^2+1}$. For intersection to exist, we need $\sqrt{3}-\sqrt{k^2+1} < \sqrt{k^2+1}-|k|$.
    - $\Rightarrow \sqrt{3} + |k| < 2\sqrt{k^2+1}$.
    - Square both sides: $3 + k^2 + 2\sqrt{3}|k| < 4(k^2+1) = 4k^2+4$.
    - $\Rightarrow 2\sqrt{3}|k| < 3k^2 + 1$.
    - Let $t = |k| \ge 0$. $3t^2 - 2\sqrt{3}t + 1 > 0$. Discriminant $\Delta = 12 - 12 = 0$. So $3t^2 - 2\sqrt{3}t + 1 = (\sqrt{3}t - 1)^2 \ge 0$. It's strictly positive except when $t = 1/\sqrt{3}$.
    - So for $|k| \ne 1/\sqrt{3}$, intersection is non-empty. At $|k| = 1/\sqrt{3}$, equality holds, meaning the intervals just touch? Let's check carefully.
    - Actually, we need strict inequality for intersection to have interior points (since $d_i < 1$). If they just touch, there's only one $b$, but we need $d_i < 1$. Wait, if intervals touch at a point, that point gives $d_i=1$ for some circle? Let's check $k=1/\sqrt{3}$.
    - $\sqrt{k^2+1} = \sqrt{4/3} = 2/\sqrt{3}$. $|k|=1/\sqrt{3}$.
    - $I_1 = (-2/\sqrt{3}+1/\sqrt{3}, 2/\sqrt{3}-1/\sqrt{3}) = (-1/\sqrt{3}, 1/\sqrt{3})$.
    - $I_3 = (\sqrt{3}-2/\sqrt{3}, \sqrt{3}+2/\sqrt{3}) = (1/\sqrt{3}, 5/\sqrt{3})$.
    - They touch at $b=1/\sqrt{3}$. At this $b$, $d_1 = \frac{|1/\sqrt{3}-1/\sqrt{3}|}{2/\sqrt{3}} = 0 < 1$. $d_2 = \frac{|1/\sqrt{3}+1/\sqrt{3}|}{2/\sqrt{3}} = 1$. So $d_2=1$, tangent, not two intersections. So $k=1/\sqrt{3}$ is not allowed.
    - Thus, $k$ cannot be $1/\sqrt{3}$ or $-1/\sqrt{3}$. So $k$ cannot take *any* real number. Option A is **False**.
    - Wait, let's re-verify the condition $3t^2 - 2\sqrt{3}t + 1 > 0$. It's $(\sqrt{3}t-1)^2 > 0$. So it fails exactly at $t=1/\sqrt{3}$. So $k \ne \pm 1/\sqrt{3}$. Thus A is false.

4.  **Evaluate Option B: Exactly 3 lines with $s_1 = s_2 = s_3$.**
    - $s_1 = s_2 = s_3 \iff d_1 = d_2 = d_3$ (since $s_i = 2\sqrt{1-d_i^2}$ is monotonic in $d_i$).
    - $d_1 = d_2 \Rightarrow |b-k| = |b+k| \Rightarrow b=0$ or $k=0$.
    - Case 1: $b=0$. Then $d_1 = \frac{|k|}{\sqrt{k^2+1}}$, $d_2 = \frac{|k|}{\sqrt{k^2+1}}$, $d_3 = \frac{\sqrt{3}}{\sqrt{k^2+1}}$.
      - $d_1 = d_3 \Rightarrow |k| = \sqrt{3} \Rightarrow k = \pm \sqrt{3}$.
      - Check validity: For $k=\sqrt{3}, b=0$, $d_1 = \frac{\sqrt{3}}{2} < 1$, $d_3 = \frac{\sqrt{3}}{2} < 1$. Valid.
      - So two lines: $y = \sqrt{3}x$ and $y = -\sqrt{3}x$.
    - Case 2: $k=0$. Then $d_1 = |b|$, $d_2 = |b|$, $d_3 = \frac{|b-\sqrt{3}|}{1} = |b-\sqrt{3}|$.
      - $d_1 = d_3 \Rightarrow |b| = |b-\sqrt{3}| \Rightarrow b = \frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}$.
      - Check validity: $d_1 = \frac{\sqrt{3}}{2} < 1$. Valid.
      - So one line: $y = \frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}$.
    - Total lines: 3. Option B is **True**.
    - Wait, is there any other case? $d_1=d_2=d_3$ implies distances from three centers to line are equal. The line must be parallel to the sides of the triangle formed by centers, or pass through the incenter/excenters? Actually, the set of points equidistant to two circles (same radius) is the perpendicular bisector of the segment connecting centers. Here radii are equal, so line must be parallel to $O_1O_2$ (x-axis) or equidistant to $O_1$ and $O_2$ means it's horizontal ($k=0$) or vertical? Wait, distance from $(-1,0)$ and $(1,0)$ to $y=kx+b$ are equal $\Rightarrow |b-k| = |b+k| \Rightarrow b=0$ or $k=0$. Correct.
    - So exactly 3 lines. B is correct.

5.  **Evaluate Option C: $s_1 + s_2 + s_3 = 3$ has more than 3 solutions.**
    - $s_i = 2\sqrt{1-d_i^2}$. Sum = 3.
    - Since $s_i \le 2$ (max chord is diameter), sum $\le 6$.
    - $s_1+s_2+s_3=3$ is a specific level set.
    - Let's analyze geometrically or algebraically.
    - $s_i = 3 \Rightarrow$ average chord length is 1.
    - Note that $s=2$ when $d=0$ (line passes through center). $s=0$ when $d=1$.
    - Consider lines passing through centers. If $l$ passes through $O_1$, $d_1=0, s_1=2$. Then we need $s_2+s_3=1$.
    - Is the set of solutions a curve in $(k,b)$ plane? The condition defines a curve. The question says "lines $l$ ... more than 3". Usually, if it's a continuous curve, there are infinitely many. But maybe constraints restrict it to discrete points?
    - Let's check specific cases.
    - Symmetry: The configuration of circles is symmetric about the y-axis? $C_1$ and $C_2$ are symmetric about y-axis. $C_3$ is on y-axis. So the whole system is symmetric about $x=0$.
    - If $l: y=kx+b$ is a solution, then $y=-kx+b$ is also a solution (due to symmetry). So solutions come in pairs unless $k=0$.
    - Let's test $k=0$ (horizontal lines $y=b$).
      - $d_1 = |b|$, $d_2 = |b|$, $d_3 = |b-\sqrt{3}|$.
      - $s_1 = s_2 = 2\sqrt{1-b^2}$, $s_3 = 2\sqrt{1-(b-\sqrt{3})^2}$.
      - Need $4\sqrt{1-b^2} + 2\sqrt{1-(b-\sqrt{3})^2} = 3$.
      - Domain: $|b|<1$ and $|b-\sqrt{3}|<1 \Rightarrow \sqrt{3}-1 < b < \sqrt{3}+1$. Intersection: $\sqrt{3}-1 < b < 1$. ($\sqrt{3}\approx 1.732$, so $0.732 < b < 1$).
      - Let $f(b) = 4\sqrt{1-b^2} + 2\sqrt{1-(b-\sqrt{3})^2}$.
      - At $b=1$, $f(1) = 0 + 2\sqrt{1-(1-\sqrt{3})^2} = 2\sqrt{1-(4-2\sqrt{3})} = 2\sqrt{2\sqrt{3}-3} \approx 2\sqrt{0.464} \approx 1.36 < 3$.
      - At $b=\sqrt{3}-1 \approx 0.732$, $f(\sqrt{3}-1) = 4\sqrt{1-(\sqrt{3}-1)^2} + 0 = 4\sqrt{1-(4-2\sqrt{3})} = 4\sqrt{2\sqrt{3}-3} \approx 2.72 < 3$.
      - Maximum of $f(b)$ in this interval? Derivative might show it's less than 3. Actually, max possible sum for horizontal lines is when $b$ balances them. But clearly $<3$. So no horizontal lines satisfy sum=3.
    - Let's test lines through origin ($b=0$). $y=kx$.
      - $d_1 = \frac{|k|}{\sqrt{k^2+1}}$, $d_2 = \frac{|k|}{\sqrt{k^2+1}}$, $d_3 = \frac{\sqrt{3}}{\sqrt{k^2+1}}$.
      - $s_1=s_2 = 2\sqrt{1-\frac{k^2}{k^2+1}} = 2\sqrt{\frac{1}{k^2+1}} = \frac{2}{\sqrt{k^2+1}}$.
      - $s_3 = 2\sqrt{1-\frac{3}{k^2+1}} = 2\sqrt{\frac{k^2-2}{k^2+1}}$. (Requires $k^2 \ge 2$).
      - Sum: $\frac{4}{\sqrt{k^2+1}} + 2\sqrt{\frac{k^2-2}{k^2+1}} = 3$.
      - Let $u = \sqrt{k^2+1} \ge \sqrt{3}$. Then $k^2-2 = u^2-3$.
      - $\frac{4}{u} + 2\sqrt{\frac{u^2-3}{u^2}} = 3 \Rightarrow \frac{4}{u} + \frac{2\sqrt{u^2-3}}{u} = 3 \Rightarrow 4 + 2\sqrt{u^2-3} = 3u$.
      - $2\sqrt{u^2-3} = 3u - 4$. Square: $4(u^2-3) = 9u^2 - 24u + 16 \Rightarrow 4u^2 - 12 = 9u^2 - 24u + 16 \Rightarrow 5u^2 - 24u + 28 = 0$.
      - Discriminant: $576 - 560 = 16$. Roots: $u = \frac{24 \pm 4}{10} = 2.8$ or $2$.
      - $u \ge \sqrt{3} \approx 1.732$. Both are valid? Check original equation: $3u-4 \ge 0 \Rightarrow u \ge 4/3$. Both ok.
      - $u=2 \Rightarrow k^2+1=4 \Rightarrow k^2=3 \Rightarrow k=\pm\sqrt{3}$.
      - $u=2.8 = 14/5 \Rightarrow k^2 = 196/25 - 1 = 171/25 \Rightarrow k = \pm \sqrt{171}/5$.
      - So for $b=0$, we have 4 lines. Plus symmetry, maybe more?
      - Wait, the question says "lines $l$ ... more than 3". We already found 4 lines with $b=0$. So definitely more than 3. Thus C is **True**.
      - Let's double check this calculation.
      - $u=2 \Rightarrow k^2=3$. $s_1=s_2=2/2=1$. $s_3=2\sqrt{(3-2)/4}=1$. Sum=3. Correct. (These are the lines from option B!)
      - $u=2.8 \Rightarrow k^2=6.84$. $s_1=s_2=4/2.8=10/7 \approx 1.428$. $s_3=2\sqrt{(6.84-2)/7.84}=2\sqrt{4.84/7.84}=2(2.2/2.8)=11/7 \approx 1.571$. Sum = $20/7+11/7=31/7 \ne 3$. Wait, calculation error?
      - Let's recompute $u=14/5$: $4/u = 20/14 = 10/7$. $\sqrt{u^2-3}/u = \sqrt{(196/25-75/25)} / (14/5) = \sqrt{121/25} / (14/5) = (11/5)/(14/5) = 11/14$. So $2\sqrt{\dots}/u = 22/14 = 11/7$. Sum = $20/14 + 22/14 = 42/14 = 3$. Correct.
      - So $k=\pm\sqrt{3}$ and $k=\pm\frac{\sqrt{171}}{5}$ are 4 solutions with $b=0$.
      - Thus, there are at least 4 lines. So "more than 3" is true. C is **True**.

6.  **Evaluate Option D: When $b=0$, max of $s_1+s_2+s_3$ is $\frac{2\sqrt{21}}{3}$.**
    - We already analyzed $b=0$. $y=kx$.
    - $S(k) = s_1+s_2+s_3 = \frac{4}{\sqrt{k^2+1}} + 2\sqrt{1-\frac{3}{k^2+1}}$.
    - Let $t = \frac{1}{\sqrt{k^2+1}}$. Since $k \in \mathbb{R}$, $t \in (0, 1]$.
    - But we need intersection with $C_3$, so $d_3 < 1 \Rightarrow \frac{\sqrt{3}}{\sqrt{k^2+1}} < 1 \Rightarrow \sqrt{k^2+1} > \sqrt{3} \Rightarrow t < \frac{1}{\sqrt{3}}$.
    - So $t \in (0, 1/\sqrt{3})$.
    - $S(t) = 4t + 2\sqrt{1-3t^2}$.
    - Find max of $S(t)$ on $(0, 1/\sqrt{3})$.
    - $S'(t) = 4 + 2 \cdot \frac{1}{2\sqrt{1-3t^2}} \cdot (-6t) = 4 - \frac{6t}{\sqrt{1-3t^2}}$.
    - Set $S'(t)=0 \Rightarrow 4 = \frac{6t}{\sqrt{1-3t^2}} \Rightarrow 2\sqrt{1-3t^2} = 3t$.
    - Square: $4(1-3t^2) = 9t^2 \Rightarrow 4 - 12t^2 = 9t^2 \Rightarrow 21t^2 = 4 \Rightarrow t^2 = 4/21 \Rightarrow t = \frac{2}{\sqrt{21}}$.
    - Check if $t < 1/\sqrt{3}$: $4/21 \approx 0.19$, $1/3 \approx 0.33$. Yes, valid.
    - Max value: $S_{max} = 4\left(\frac{2}{\sqrt{21}}\right) + 2\sqrt{1 - 3\left(\frac{4}{21}\right)} = \frac{8}{\sqrt{21}} + 2\sqrt{1 - \frac{12}{21}} = \frac{8}{\sqrt{21}} + 2\sqrt{\frac{9}{21}} = \frac{8}{\sqrt{21}} + \frac{6}{\sqrt{21}} = \frac{14}{\sqrt{21}} = \frac{14\sqrt{21}}{21} = \frac{2\sqrt{21}}{3}$.
    - Matches option D exactly. So D is **True**.

7.  **Re-evaluate Option A carefully:**
    - I concluded A is False because $k \ne \pm 1/\sqrt{3}$.
    - Let's verify the condition for $k$ again.
    - We need existence of $b$ such that $d_1, d_2, d_3 < 1$.
    - $d_1 < 1 \iff (b-k)^2 < k^2+1 \iff b^2 - 2bk < 1$.
    - $d_2 < 1 \iff (b+k)^2 < k^2+1 \iff b^2 + 2bk < 1$.
    - Sum: $2b^2 < 2 \Rightarrow |b| < 1$.
    - From $b^2 \pm 2bk < 1$, we get $|2bk| < 1-b^2$. If $b=0$, this is $0<1$, always true. So $b=0$ works for any $k$? Wait, if $b=0$, $d_3 = \frac{\sqrt{3}}{\sqrt{k^2+1}}$. For $d_3 < 1$, we need $\sqrt{k^2+1} > \sqrt{3} \Rightarrow k^2 > 2$.
    - So for $b=0$, we need $|k| > \sqrt{2}$.
    - What if $b \ne 0$? The condition $|2bk| < 1-b^2$ implies $|k| < \frac{1-b^2}{2|b|}$. The max of RHS over $b \in (0,1)$ is when derivative is 0? Let $f(b) = \frac{1-b^2}{2b}$. $f'(b) = \frac{-2b(2b) - 2(1-b^2)}{4b^2} = \frac{-4b^2 - 2 + 2b^2}{4b^2} = \frac{-2b^2-2}{4b^2} < 0$. So $f(b)$ decreases from $\infty$ to 0. So for any $k$, we can pick small enough $b$ such that $|k| < f(b)$. So the $d_1, d_2 < 1$ conditions can be satisfied for any $k$ by choosing small $b$.
    - But we also need $d_3 < 1$: $|b-\sqrt{3}| < \sqrt{k^2+1}$.
    - Since $|b|<1$, $b-\sqrt{3} \in (\sqrt{3}-1, -\sqrt{3}-1)$? Wait, $b \in (-1, 1)$, so $b-\sqrt{3} \in (\sqrt{3}-1, -\sqrt{3}-1)$ is wrong. $b-\sqrt{3} \in (-\sqrt{3}-1, 1-\sqrt{3})$. Both are negative. $|b-\sqrt{3}| = \sqrt{3}-b$.
    - So we need $\sqrt{3}-b < \sqrt{k^2+1}$. Since $b < 1$, $\sqrt{3}-b > \sqrt{3}-1 \approx 0.732$.
    - We need $\sqrt{k^2+1} > \sqrt{3}-b$. Since $b$ can be chosen close to 1, $\sqrt{3}-b$ can be close to $\sqrt{3}-1$. So we need $\sqrt{k^2+1} > \sqrt{3}-1 \approx 0.732$, which is always true since $\sqrt{k^2+1} \ge 1$.
    - Wait, this suggests for *any* $k$, we can find a $b$ (e.g., $b$ close to 1) such that all conditions hold?
    - Let's test $k=0$. We need $d_1=|b|<1$, $d_2=|b|<1$, $d_3=|b-\sqrt{3}|<1$.
    - $|b-\sqrt{3}|<1 \Rightarrow \sqrt{3}-1 < b < \sqrt{3}+1$. Combined with $|b|<1$, we get $b \in (\sqrt{3}-1, 1)$. This interval is non-empty. So $k=0$ is possible.
    - Let's test $k=1/\sqrt{3} \approx 0.577$.
    - Conditions: $b \in (-\sqrt{4/3}+1/\sqrt{3}, \sqrt{4/3}-1/\sqrt{3}) = (-1/\sqrt{3}, 1/\sqrt{3})$.
    - And $|b-\sqrt{3}| < \sqrt{4/3} = 2/\sqrt{3} \Rightarrow b \in (\sqrt{3}-2/\sqrt{3}, \sqrt{3}+2/\sqrt{3}) = (1/\sqrt{3}, 5/\sqrt{3})$.
    - Intersection of $(-1/\sqrt{3}, 1/\sqrt{3})$ and $(1/\sqrt{3}, 5/\sqrt{3})$ is empty. They touch at $1/\sqrt{3}$, but open intervals don't include it. So no $b$ exists for $k=1/\sqrt{3}$.
    - So $k$ cannot be $1/\sqrt{3}$. Thus A is indeed **False**.
    - My previous analysis holds. A is False.

    Wait, let's re-read A: "k可以取任意实数" (k can take any real number). This usually means the set of possible k for valid lines is $\mathbb{R}$. Since $k=\pm 1/\sqrt{3}$ are excluded, A is false.

    Let's double check this exclusion. Is it possible that for $k=1/\sqrt{3}$, there is another $b$ outside those intervals?
    $d_1 < 1 \Rightarrow (b-k)^2 < k^2+1 \Rightarrow b^2 - 2bk < 1$.
    $d_2 < 1 \Rightarrow b^2 + 2bk < 1$.
    These two imply $b^2 + 2b|k| < 1$. Roots of $b^2 + 2|k|b - 1 = 0$ are $-|k| \pm \sqrt{k^2+1}$. So $b \in (-|k|-\sqrt{k^2+1}, -|k|+\sqrt{k^2+1})$? No, parabola opens up, so between roots.
    Actually, $b^2 - 2|k||b| < 1$ is easier. Let $B=|b|$. $B^2 - 2|k|B - 1 < 0$. Roots: $|k| \pm \sqrt{k^2+1}$. So $B \in (0, |k|+\sqrt{k^2+1})$. Wait, this is always true for small B.
    Let's solve strictly:
    $(b-k)^2 < k^2+1 \Rightarrow b^2 - 2bk + k^2 < k^2+1 \Rightarrow b^2 - 2bk - 1 < 0$.
    $(b+k)^2 < k^2+1 \Rightarrow b^2 + 2bk - 1 < 0$.
    Add: $2b^2 - 2 < 0 \Rightarrow b^2 < 1 \Rightarrow |b| < 1$.
    From $b^2 - 2bk < 1$ and $b^2 + 2bk < 1$, we get $b^2 + 2|b||k| < 1$.
    Let $u = |b| \in [0, 1)$. $u^2 + 2|k|u - 1 < 0$.
    Roots for $u$: $-|k| \pm \sqrt{k^2+1}$. Positive root is $\sqrt{k^2+1}-|k|$.
    So we need $u < \sqrt{k^2+1}-|k|$.
    So $|b| < \sqrt{k^2+1}-|k|$.
    This interval is $(-\Delta, \Delta)$ where $\Delta = \sqrt{k^2+1}-|k|$.
    Third condition: $(b-\sqrt{3})^2 < k^2+1 \Rightarrow |b-\sqrt{3}| < \sqrt{k^2+1}$.
    Since $b \in (-\Delta, \Delta)$ and $\Delta < 1$ (as $\sqrt{k^2+1} < |k|+1$), $b < 1 < \sqrt{3}$. So $b-\sqrt{3} < 0$.
    Thus $\sqrt{3}-b < \sqrt{k^2+1} \Rightarrow b > \sqrt{3}-\sqrt{k^2+1}$.
    So we need intersection of $(-\Delta, \Delta)$ and $(\sqrt{3}-\sqrt{k^2+1}, \infty)$ to be non-empty.
    Need $\sqrt{3}-\sqrt{k^2+1} < \Delta = \sqrt{k^2+1}-|k|$.
    $\Rightarrow \sqrt{3} + |k| < 2\sqrt{k^2+1}$.
    Square: $3 + k^2 + 2\sqrt{3}|k| < 4k^2 + 4 \Rightarrow 3k^2 - 2\sqrt{3}|k| + 1 > 0 \Rightarrow (\sqrt{3}|k|-1)^2 > 0$.
    Strict inequality fails at $|k|=1/\sqrt{3}$. So for $k=\pm 1/\sqrt{3}$, no $b$ exists.
    For all other $k$, the inequality holds strictly, so there exists an interval of $b$.
    Thus $k \in \mathbb{R} \setminus \{\pm 1/\sqrt{3}\}$.
    So A is False. Correct.

8.  **Final Check of Options:**
    - A: False.
    - B: True. (3 lines: $y=\sqrt{3}x, y=-\sqrt{3}x, y=\sqrt{3}/2$)
    - C: True. (At least 4 lines with $b=0$ satisfy sum=3, plus others likely exist. The question says "more than 3", which is satisfied.)
    - D: True. (Max value calculation matches exactly.)

    Wait, is it possible that C implies something else? "满足s1+s2+s3=3的直线l多于3条". We found exactly 4 lines with $b=0$. Are there more with $b \ne 0$? Probably yes, but 4 > 3 is enough. So C is True.
    Let's quickly verify if $s_1+s_2+s_3=3$ could have exactly 3 lines? We found 4 for $b=0$ alone. So definitely >3.
    One detail: for $b=0$, we solved $4/u + 2\sqrt{(u^2-3)/u^2} = 3$ and got $u=2$ and $u=2.8$. Both give valid $k$. So 4 lines. Correct.
    What about symmetry? $y=kx$ and $y=-kx$ are distinct unless $k=0$. Here $k \ne 0$. So 4 distinct lines.
    So C is unambiguously True.

    Let's re-verify D's maximum value derivation.
    $S(t) = 4t + 2\sqrt{1-3t^2}$, $t \in (0, 1/\sqrt{3})$.

Prompt
请解答以下数学题（填空题）。逐步推理，将最终答案放在 \boxed{} 中。

12. 双曲线5x^2 - 6y^2 = 1的离心率为__________.

请将答案放在 \boxed{} 中。
Response
要解答这个问题，我们需要将双曲线的方程化为标准形式，然后利用双曲线的性质求出离心率。

**第一步：将方程化为标准形式**

双曲线的标准方程（焦点在 $x$ 轴上）通常写作：
$$ \frac{x^2}{a^2} - \frac{y^2}{b^2} = 1 $$
其中 $a > 0, b > 0$。

给定的方程为：
$$ 5x^2 - 6y^2 = 1 $$

为了将其转化为标准形式，我们将方程变形为：
$$ \frac{x^2}{\frac{1}{5}} - \frac{y^2}{\frac{1}{6}} = 1 $$

**第二步：确定参数 $a^2$ 和 $b^2$**

通过对比标准方程，我们可以确定：
$$ a^2 = \frac{1}{5} $$
$$ b^2 = \frac{1}{6} $$

**第三步：计算离心率 $e$**

双曲线的离心率公式为 $e = \frac{c}{a}$，其中 $c$ 是半焦距，满足关系式 $c^2 = a^2 + b^2$。
或者直接使用离心率与 $a, b$ 的关系公式：
$$ e = \sqrt{1 + \frac{b^2}{a^2}} $$

我们将 $a^2$ 和 $b^2$ 的值代入公式：

1.  计算 $\frac{b^2}{a^2}$：
    $$ \frac{b^2}{a^2} = \frac{\frac{1}{6}}{\frac{1}{5}} = \frac{1}{6} \times \frac{5}{1} = \frac{5}{6} $$

2.  计算 $e$：
    $$ e = \sqrt{1 + \frac{5}{6}} $$
    $$ e = \sqrt{\frac{6}{6} + \frac{5}{6}} $$
    $$ e = \sqrt{\frac{11}{6}} $$

**第四步：化简结果**

为了使答案更规范，我们需要对分母进行有理化：
$$ e = \frac{\sqrt{11}}{\sqrt{6}} = \frac{\sqrt{11} \times \sqrt{6}}{\sqrt{6} \times \sqrt{6}} = \frac{\sqrt{66}}{6} $$

因此，该双曲线的离心率为 $\frac{\sqrt{66}}{6}$。

\boxed{\frac{\sqrt{66}}{6}}

Here's my thought process for solving this math problem:

1.  **Analyze the Request:**
    *   **Problem:** Find the eccentricity of the hyperbola given by the equation $5x^2 - 6y^2 = 1$.
    *   **Format:** Fill-in-the-blank (implies a specific value or expression).
    *   **Output Requirement:** Step-by-step reasoning, with the final answer in `\boxed{}`.

2.  **Recall the Properties of a Hyperbola:**
    *   **Standard Form:** A hyperbola centered at the origin with foci on the x-axis has the standard equation $\frac{x^2}{a^2} - \frac{y^2}{b^2} = 1$, where $a > 0$ and $b > 0$.
    *   **Parameters:**
        *   $a$: length of the semi-major (transverse) axis.
        *   $b$: length of the semi-minor (conjugate) axis.
        *   $c$: distance from the center to a focus.
    *   **Relationship:** For a hyperbola, $c^2 = a^2 + b^2$.
    *   **Eccentricity ($e$):** The eccentricity is defined as the ratio of the focal distance to the semi-major axis length, i.e., $e = \frac{c}{a}$.
    *   **Alternative Formula:** Using $c^2 = a^2 + b^2$, we can express $e$ directly in terms of $a$ and $b$:
        $e = \sqrt{\frac{a^2 + b^2}{a^2}} = \sqrt{1 + \frac{b^2}{a^2}}$.

    *   Equation: $5x^2 - 6y^2 = 1$.

    4.  Recall the formula for eccentricity $e$ of a hyperbola: $e = \sqrt{1 + \frac{b^2}{a^2}}$. Alternatively, calculate $c$ first where $c^2 = a^2 + b^2$ and then $e = c/a$.
    5.  Compute the ratio $\frac{b^2}{a^2}$:
        $$ \frac{b^2}{a^2} = \frac{1/6}{1/5} = \frac{5}{6} $$
    6.  Compute $e$:
        $$ e = \sqrt{1 + \frac{5}{6}} = \sqrt{\frac{11}{6}} $$
    7.  Rationalize the denominator to get the standard answer format.
        $$ e = \frac{\sqrt{11}}{\sqrt{6}} = \frac{\sqrt{66}}{6} $$

    The result is $\frac{\sqrt{66}}{6}$.
